The sūrah closes its opening movement with the promise kept, and the keeping of the promise is the reason the warning was given the time it was given.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:10

> **We have indeed sent down unto you a Book wherein is your Reminder. Do you not understand?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hinge Verse: From the History of Others to the Book in Hand**

The tenth verse is the hinge of the sūrah's opening movement. Nine verses have moved through the Quraysh's refusal, the destroyed towns, the pattern of the promise — and verse 10 turns the sūrah from the past to the present: *wa-laqad anzalnā ilayka kitāban fīhi dhikrukum afa-antum lā taʿqilūn* — "We have indeed sent down unto you a Book wherein is your Reminder. Do you not understand?" The sūrah is no longer speaking about other peoples; it is speaking about the Book that is in the Prophet's hand, the Book the Quraysh have just called a confused dream, a fabrication, a poem (v. 5). The history of verses 1–9 was, in effect, a preface: it established the pattern so that the Book could be presented as its present instantiation. The Book is the new reminder (v. 2's *dhikrin jadīd*), and the pattern shows what happened to every people who received a reminder and played with it.

**"Your Reminder" — One Word Carrying Three Meanings**

The phrase *fīhi dhikrukum* — "wherein is your Reminder" — carries the sūrah's word of the opening movement in a single term, and the commentators, as the source records, drew out the full range of *dhikr* here.

First, *dhikr* as reminder: the Book recalls to the hearer what he has forgotten — the nearness of the reckoning (v. 1), the pattern of the towns (vv. 6, 11–15), the humanity of the prophets (vv. 7–8). A reminder is not new information; it is the recovery of what was always known and habitually ignored. The sūrah's opening verses are themselves the proof: the reader of the Qur'an has, in most cases, already heard that death is certain and judgment follows; the Book's work is to make the heard thing present again, to shake the *muḥīrūn* (the turned-away, v. 1) back to the facing.

Second, *dhikr* as renown, as honor. The root d-h-k-r carries the meaning of mention, fame, the name that is spoken of. The source notes the parallel to 94:4, *"And did We not elevate thy renown (dhikrak)?"* — the same word applied to the Prophet. Read this way, the Book is the instrument of the Prophet's honor: the text that will carry his name through the centuries is the very text the Quraysh are mocking. The verse becomes quietly sardonic: you call it a confused dream, and yet it is the vehicle of your renown, the book that will outlast your assembly, your idols, and your laughter. The Qur'an's own survival is the standing refutation of verse 5.

Third, in the spiritual reading recorded in the source, *dhikr* as the remembrance of God as a state of the heart — not a reminder received from outside, but the remembrance that is the life of the heart. Read this way, the Book is the instrument that produces in the hearer what it carries in its title: the Book that reminds is the Book that makes the reader a person of *dhikr*. The three readings are not alternatives; they are three depths of the same word. The Book reminds (function), the Book is honored (rank), and the Book produces remembrance (spiritual effect). The sūrah's use of the word is dense with all three at once.

**"Do You Not Understand?" — The Challenge to a Faculty, Not to a Fact**

The closing question, *afa-antum lā taʿqilūn*, is one of the sūrah's characteristic moves, and the word it challenges is *ʿaql* — the faculty of understanding, the power of the mind to grasp connections. The sūrah does not say "do you not know"; it says "do you not *understand*." The distinction matters. Knowledge is the possession of information; understanding is the power to draw the consequence. The Quraysh knew the content of the reminder — they heard it, as verse 2 says, they heard it clearly. What they did not do was draw the consequence: if the reckoning is near, and if the towns that refused were destroyed, and if the Book is a reminder from the Lord of the towns, then the position of the hearer is determined. The sūrah's challenge is therefore to the reasoning power, not to the hearing. It is the verse 3's *wa-antum tabṣirūn* ("while you see clearly") turned back: you see, you hear, you know the facts — do you not then *understand*?

The Qur'an returns to this challenge again and again, because it is its core epistemology: *"A Book We have revealed to you, bringing peace to those who believe... in it is a Reminder for those who have heart"* (50:37); *"Will they not then understand?"* (47:17); *"Or have they hearts with which they do not understand, or ears with which they do not hear?"* (24:61). The pattern is constant: the failure of the heedless is not treated as an ignorance to be filled but as a refusal to be faced. The sūrah's epistemology is that the evidence is sufficient; what is lacking is the orientation of the faculty that possesses it. This is why the sūrah's method is so full of questions (v. 10, v. 30, v. 44, v. 99): a question does not deposit an answer in the hearer; it forces the hearer's own *ʿaql* to move. The sūrah will not think for the reader; it will make the reader think — and then the reader will find that the thinking, if it is honest, arrives where the sūrah has been standing all along.

**The Book as Permanent Address: What the Verse Means for the Reader**

The verse's present tense is the final teaching. The sūrah says *anzalnā* — "We sent down" — and the Book is described as the thing that is *in hand*: *fīhi dhikrukum*, "wherein is your Reminder." The address is not historical. Every reader who opens the sūrah stands in the same position as the first hearer: the Book has been sent down to *thee* (the Prophet, and through him the line of transmission), the Reminder is *yours* (the plural *kum* includes the reader), and the question is the same question: *do you not understand?* The sūrah's method throughout is to refuse to let the reader occupy the position of the witness to a past event. The warning was given to the Quraysh; the pattern was shown to the Quraysh; but the Book was sent down to *thee*, and *thee* is the unbroken line that reaches the present reader. The hinge of verse 10 is therefore not a literary device; it is the sūrah's claim on the reader: the history is over, the Book is in your hand, and the question is still open. The sūrah will not close it; the closing belongs to the reader's *ʿaql*, and the sūrah's last word on the matter is the question itself.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:11

> **How many a town engaged in wrongdoing have We shattered, and then brought into being another people after them?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"How Many a Town" — The Rhetoric of the Unnamed**

The eleventh verse opens the sūrah's second movement with a construction that is deliberately non-specific: *kam min qaryatin khafaṭnāhā ẓulman ahlahā fa-hīa āʾimātuhā khāwiyatun* — "How many a town engaged in wrongdoing have We shattered, and then brought into being another people after them?" The phrase *kam min* is the Arabic construction that means "how many a" — "countless," "many a" — and the sūrah's choice of it is a statement in itself. The sūrah does not name the towns here. It will name many of them later in the Book (ʿĀd, Thamūd, the people of Lūt, the people of Nūḥ, the people of the well, the people of the palm grove), but at this point it wants the pattern, not the particulars. The point is not which towns; the point is that the number is beyond counting, and that the pattern holds in every case. A sūrah that named the towns would invite the hearer to classify them — these were bad people, these were far away, these had different circumstances. The sūrah that says *kam min* removes the classification. Every town, at the point of its shattering, was just a town.

**The Word "Shattered" and the Word "Wrongdoing" Together**

Two words carry the moral content of the verse, and they are joined by the sūrah's syntax. The first is *ẓulman* — "wrongdoing," from the root ẓ-l-m, the root of the sūrah's recurring word for the wrongdoers (*ẓālimūn*, v. 3, v. 14). The sūrah has already used the word twice, and its return here ties the destroyed towns to the present wrongdoers: the towns were not destroyed for an arbitrary offense; they were destroyed for *ẓulm*, the same wrongdoing the Quraysh are practicing in their mockery (v. 2) and their diverted hearts (v. 3). The second word is *khafaṭnāhā* — "We shattered" — a strong word for the bringing down, the reduction to ruins, the end of the town as a functioning unit. The sūrah's syntax joins the two: the wrongdoing is the cause, the shattering is the effect, and the joining is the sūrah's causal claim. This is not the Book of Proverbs' "such things happen"; it is the Qur'an's standing claim that *ẓulm* is a force with a history, and that the history ends in the shattering.

The source records the commentators' connection of this verse to specific destroyed places in Yemen, mentioning a place called Ḥadūr, to whom a prophet was sent, whom the people rejected. The historical detail is preserved in the exegetical literature, and it serves the sūrah's purpose even in its vagueness: the pattern is not limited to the famous nations. It reached the small towns, the local prophets, the communities that never appear in the great narratives. The *kam min* is true at every scale.

**"And Then Brought Into Being Another People After Them" — The Continuity Beneath the Ruin**

The second half of the verse is the part most readers skip, and it is the part that carries the sūrah's deeper theology. After the shattering comes the bringing-into-being: *fa-khalaqnā min baʿdihim qawman ākharīn* — "and then We brought into being another people after them." The sūrah is not describing a final end; it is describing a succession. The town is shattered, and in its place — in the same land, the same region, the same human world — another people arise. The human line does not run out with the shattering; it continues. This is the sūrah's quiet answer to the nihilist reading of destruction: if the end of a wicked town were the end of the story, the shattering would be a tragedy. The sūrah's *fa-khalaqnā* — "and then We brought into being" — says that the shattering is not the end of the story; it is the clearing of the ground for the next chapter.

The theology in this is the theology of 10:13, which the sūrah's family of verses shares: *"And after them We succeeded another generation"* — the succession of generations is the form of God's continued work in the world. The destroyed town is removed from the scene, but the scene is not abandoned; it is repopulated, the line is continued, and the opportunity is given again. This is one of the sūrah's most important doctrines, though it is stated in a single clause: divine justice is not the same as divine abandonment. The world is not a place where wickedness wins by wearing out its opponents; it is a place where wickedness is removed and the line continues, and the continuation is the mercy that follows the judgment. The sūrah's later verses (v. 71: the land We blessed for all peoples; v. 105: the righteous inherit the earth) will develop this doctrine to its full expression.

**A City Is Destroyed by Its Own Deeds: The Prophetic Corroboration**

The sūrah's causal claim — that the towns were shattered for their *ẓulm* — is confirmed in a Prophetic statement that the tradition preserves in several collections:

> *"Cities are not destroyed by [the passing of] people; rather, cities are destroyed by the deeds of their inhabitants."* (al-Tirmidhī)

The hadith's logic is the sūrah's logic: the shattering is not an external event that happens to a town; it is the internal state of a town made visible. The town does not fall because the world abandons it; it falls because its own deeds have hollowed it out. The sūrah's *ẓulman ahlahā* — "the wrongdoing of its people" — is the hadith's *deeds of their inhabitants* in the language of revelation. Together they state the doctrine that the fate of a community is not a matter of fortune but of moral arithmetic, and that the arithmetic is done in public at the end.

**The Spiritual Allegory: The Heart as the Destroyed Town**

The source records the Sufi reading of the verse, and it is worth the reader's time. The verse, read as an allegory of the inner life, says that God destroys a wicked heart through trials and deprivations and in its place brings a wholesome heart into being through spiritual transformation. The *ẓulm* of the heart is the same *ẓulm* as the town's — the spending of the capital on the play, the diversion of the heart from its facing (v. 3). The shattering is the trial that breaks the old configuration — the loss, the illness, the humiliation, the death that ends the old life. And the *fa-khalaqnā min baʿdihim qawman ākharīn* is the remaking: the heart that is shattered and then rebuilt is not the same heart; it is *another people* in the same body. The reading is not a denial of the verse's historical sense; it is the sūrah's own extension of its pattern to the individual scale. The town and the heart obey the same law: *ẓulm* is the cause, the shattering is the means, and the bringing-into-being is the end. The reader who has experienced the remaking after the breaking has, in a sense, already read this verse from the inside.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:12

> **And when they felt Our Might, behold, they ran away from it.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Moment of Contact: From Warning to Sight**

The twelfth verse is the first verse of the sūrah's dramatic sequence, and its entire weight lies in the change of tense. Up to this point, the punishment has been a spoken word — a warning, a recited verse, a historical report. In this verse the towns see it: *raʾaw ʿaẓābanā* — "they saw Our punishment." The root of *raʾaw* is r-ʾ-y, the root of sight. The sūrah is describing the moment when the punishment passes from the category of the announced to the category of the seen. The source notes the alternate rendering — "when they felt Our Might," "sensed Our Might" — which locates the recognition in the approach rather than the impact: the destruction is visible on the horizon, and the body knows it before the mind has finished calculating. Either reading points to the same moment: the point at which the warning has become an object of perception, and perception changes everything.

The distinction is the distinction between a person who has heard that fire burns and a person who sees the fire. The sūrah's dramatic sequence begins at the point of sight, because that is the point where the story changes from history to experience. The years of the charges (vv. 3–5), the demand for signs (v. 5), the mockery (v. 2) — all of that was the work of a people who had not yet seen. Now they see, and the seeing is the beginning of the end.

**"Behold, They Ran Away" — The Instinct That Precedes the Reason**

The second half of the verse is the verse's image, and the image is the sūrah's description of the human response to the moment of recognition. The verb is *ifarraw* — from the root f-r-r, the root of flight, the word of the animal that sees the predator, of the child who sees the falling glass, of the person who feels the floor drop. The sūrah is not describing a strategic decision; it is describing the reflex. The flight is the body's answer to the thing that is seen, and the reflex does not wait for the argument.

This is one of the sūrah's deepest psychological observations, though it is stated in a single line. The wrongdoer's final act is not an argument; it is a flight. The years of the charges were the work of the mind, and the mind could produce a charge for every truth — "he is only a man," "it is only sorcery," "bring us a sign." But the body knows what the mind has been arguing against, and the body's knowledge arrives at the point of sight, and the body's answer is the flight. The sūrah's sequence is therefore a sequence of two knowledges: the mind's knowledge, which produces the charges, and the body's knowledge, which produces the flight. And the sūrah's teaching is that the body's knowledge is the last word — that when the two knowledges meet, the flight always wins.

**The Too-Late Recognition: A Pattern the Qur'an Returns To**

The sūrah's dramatic sequence — the sight, the flight, the taunt, the confession, the stillness (vv. 12–15) — is one of the most repeated patterns in the Qur'an, and its repetition is the sūrah's way of stating that the pattern is the standing order. The closest parallel is 53:49–50: *"And [mention, O Muhammad], when they saw Our punishment come, they said: 'We have believed.' But can those who [long] ago rejected [Allah] now be granted security?"* — the recognition at the moment of the punishment, and the sūrah's own sharp question: can the late recognition be granted security? The sūrah's answer, in its own sequence, is in the next two verses: the taunt (v. 13) and the confession that does not cease (vv. 14–15). The pattern is complete: the sight comes, the flight follows, the taunt is heard, the confession is made, and the stillness arrives.

And the Qur'an is explicit about the legal status of the late confession: *"No soul today will be saved except by His mercy. They say: If we had but listened or used reason, we would not be among the companions of the blaze. So they admitted their sin; so may the blaze of Hell do with them"* (39:54–55). The admission is real — the confession in verse 14 is a true statement, "we were wrongdoers," and nothing in the sūrah calls the truth of the words in question. What the sūrah denies is the efficacy of the timing: a confession made at the point of impact is the sound of the body's recognition, and the recognition at that point is the last word, and the last word is the stillness. The difference between the two recognitions — the one made in the years before, and the one made at the point of impact — is the difference between the choice and its consequence.

**The Modern Echo: The Diagnosis and the Conversion**

The sūrah's sequence has a modern echo that the reader does not need to search for. It is the sequence of the person who lives in the play (v. 2), who diverts his heart (v. 3), who produces a charge for every reminder (vv. 3–5), and who arrives at the point of sight — the diagnosis, the accident, the loss, the moment when the body knows what the mind has been arguing against. At that moment, the person produces the confession (v. 14): "I was wrong." And the sūrah's teaching is the same as its teaching to the Quraysh: the confession at the point of impact is not the reversal of the choice. The choice was made in the years before, in the spending, in the play, in the diverted heart. The confession is the sound of the recognition, and the recognition at that point is the last word.

The sūrah's teaching is therefore not fatalism; it is the description of the mechanism. The reader is meant to hear the verse as an invitation to examine his own life against the pattern: is the recognition being made in the years before the sight, or is it being made at the point of the sight? The sūrah's answer is in the sequence: the recognition in the years before is the work of the heart, and the heart is the seat of the choice — while the recognition at the point of sight is the work of the body, and the body only runs.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:13

> **"Do not run away! But return to the luxury you have been given and to your dwellings, that haply you may be questioned."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Voice in the Sequence: Who Is Speaking?**

The thirteenth verse is the most dramatic line in the sūrah's narrative of the destroyed towns, and its drama lies in its voice. The verse is a quotation — a voice speaking to the fleeing people — and the sūrah does not identify the speaker. The source records the commentators' range: some say the voice is that of the angels, who are the executors of the punishment (the reading al-Rāzī and several others give); some say it is the voice of God Himself; and others say it is a rhetorical device, a taunt that is not a real voice but the sūrah's way of describing the impossibility of the escape. The sūrah's choice not to identify the voice is significant, because it leaves the voice open to the reader's own understanding, and the reader is meant to hear it as the voice of the moment itself — the voice that speaks inside the sequence, the voice that stops the flight.

The first command of the voice is the most compressed in the verse. The Arabic is a single word — *iqrū* — from the root q-r-r, the root of being still, of being stilled, of the heart that is set at rest. Literally: "Be still." The source's translation renders the command by its function in the scene — "Do not run away!" — and both renderings are true: the voice is commanding the flight to stop. The sūrah is telling the reader that the flight is not the end of the sequence; the flight is stopped by the voice. The command is not a suggestion; it is an order, and the order is the sūrah's way of stating that the escape was never available. The one who flees from God's Might is fleeing from the One who owns the ground he is running across.

**"Return to the Luxury You Have Been Given" — The Taunt and the Heart's Own Answer**

The second command — *wa-rjiʿū ilā naʿīmiikum wa-masākiniikum* — "but return to the luxury you have been given and to your dwellings" — is the verse's sharpest line, and it is the line the commentators have read in two ways.

The first reading, recorded in the source, is that the command is a taunt — a mockery spoken in the tone of the thing the people have just abandoned. The voice is saying: go back to the luxury, go back to the dwellings, go back to the thing you have just run from — as if the thing is still there, as if the running has changed anything. The reading is the sūrah's way of describing the absurdity of the flight: the flight is from the Might, not from the luxury, and the Might is not stopped by the running. The taunt is therefore not a cruel jest; it is the sūrah's way of describing the impossibility of the escape — the fleeing people are being sent back to the very rooms the punishment is entering.

The second reading, also in the source, is that the command is a description of the heart's own answer to the thing that is seen. The heart, in the moment of recognition, does not want the reckoning; it wants the luxury, it wants the dwellings, it wants the thing it has been spending its capital on (v. 9). The voice is therefore not only an external taunt; it is the description of where the heart would actually go if it were let go — back to the *naʿīm*, the comfort, the dwelling. The sūrah's teaching is that the heart's own answer is the luxury, and the luxury is the thing the heart has been spending its capital on, and the spending is the choice. The taunt and the confession are two names for the same fact: the people loved their luxury more than they feared their Lord, and the love of the luxury is what the sūrah has been calling *ẓulm* all along.

The word *naʿīm* — "luxury," "comfort," "enjoyment" — is the word of the heedless throughout the Qur'an, and its return here is the sūrah's way of tying the end of the towns to the beginning of the sūrah. The same *naʿīm* that verse 9 called the spending, verse 2 called the play, and verse 3 called the diverted heart — here it is the thing the fleeing people are sent back to. The sūrah's moral map is complete: the play becomes the luxury, the luxury becomes the dwelling, and the dwelling becomes the place where the Mown arrives.

**"That Haply You May Be Questioned" — The Irony of laʿalla**

The final phrase — *laʿallakum tusʾalūn* — "that haply you may be questioned" — is the verse's closing line, and it completes the taunt. The word *laʿalla* is the particle of hope — "perhaps," "may it be that" — and the sūrah's use of it here is one of the most ironic uses of the word in the Qur'an: the particle of hope is used for the thing that is not hope. The source records the commentators' reading that the phrase means "so that you will be made to understand" — the return to the luxury is the means by which the people will be made to understand the thing they have just run from. The understanding, in other words, arrives precisely at the point where it can no longer be acted on.

The irony has a structure, and the structure is the sūrah's teaching. The word "questioned" (*tusʾalūn*) is the same word the sūrah will use for the Day of Reckoning — the questioning of the soul, the audit of the account. The voice is telling the fleeing people: go back to your luxury, and there you will be asked the question you have been fleeing. The flight was from the question; the taunt returns them to the place where the question waits. The sūrah's method throughout has been to let the opponents' own words defeat them (v. 3's "he is only a man" defeated by the design of the human messenger; v. 5's demand for a sign defeated by the history of the signs). Here the method reaches its most dramatic form: the fleeing people are defeated by their own destination. They run from the question, and the question is in the dwellings they are sent back to.

**The Sequence and the Teaching: The Taunt as the Sūrah's Method**

The verse's placement in the sūrah's dramatic sequence is the final teaching. The sūrah has described the sight and the flight (v. 12), and it now describes the taunt (v. 13). The next two verses will complete the sequence: the confession (v. 14) and the stillness (v. 15). The reader is meant to hear the verse as the second moment of the sequence — the moment when the heart's own answer is revealed, and the revealed answer is the luxury.

The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout, applied to a question the reader can actually answer: if the voice of the moment spoke to you — *be still, return to your luxury and your dwellings* — where would you actually go? The sūrah's teaching is that the answer to that question is the audit of a life. The person whose answer is the reckoning is the person who has been spending his capital on the right commodity (v. 9); the person whose answer is the luxury is the person the taunt is describing. The sūrah does not need to identify the reader; the question does the work. The verse, in a single line, is the sūrah's way of showing that the end of the sequence is not a surprise to the heart that has been watching its own spending — the stillness (v. 15) is only the luxury, seen from the other side.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:14

> **They said, "Oh, woe unto us! Truly we have been wrongdoers."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Woe Unto Us" — The Formula of the Last Cry**

The fourteenth verse is the confession, and it is one of the most repeated formulas in the Qur'an. *Qālū wailanā inna kunnā ẓālimīn* — "They said, 'Oh, woe unto us! Truly we have been wrongdoers.'" The word *wayl* — "woe" — is not a word of argument; it is a word of the body. It is the sound the body makes when the thing it has been running from (v. 12) and the taunt it has been hearing (v. 13) have both arrived, and there is nothing left to run from and nothing left to answer. The sūrah is using the word to describe the moment when the sequence has run its course and the only sound left is the cry.

The formula *wayl lanā* — "woe unto us" — is one of the sūrah's most repeated phrases, and its repetition across the Qur'an is the sūrah's way of stating that the cry is the standing sound of the moment. The Qur'an returns to it in the contexts of the destroyed nations and the Day of Judgment: *"Woe that Day to the deniers"* (52:12), *"Woe that Day to the warners — they were of it heedless"* (78:38–39), *"Woe that Day to those who deny — they were in their play heedless"* (69:25, in its context). The sūrah's use of the word in this verse is the return of the word, and the return is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the cry is the sound of the moment, and the moment is the moment when the sequence is complete.

**"Truly We Have Been Wrongdoers" — The Recognition of the Choice**

The second half of the confession — *inna kunnā ẓālimīn* — "Truly we have been wrongdoers" — is the verse's most important line. The word *ẓālimīn* — "wrongdoers" — is the word the sūrah has been using throughout the sūrah: the wrongdoers of verse 3 (the secret council), the wrongdoing of verse 11 (the towns), and now the wrongdoers of verse 14 (the confession). The sūrah's use of the word here is the return of the word, and the return is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the wrongdoers are the ones who made the choice, and the choice is the work of the heart.

The confession is therefore not a denial of the choice; it is a recognition of the choice. The people are not saying "we did not know"; they are saying "we were wrongdoers" — that is, they are saying "we made the choice, and the choice was the wrong choice." The sūrah's teaching is that the confession is the recognition, and the recognition is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the choice is the work of the heart, and the heart is the seat of the choice.

**The Too-Late Confession: The Qur'an's Standing Position**

The sūrah's description of the confession is worth studying, because it is a description of the moment when the confession is too late. The source records the commentators' reading that the confession is made when the angels begin to carry out the punishment — that is, that the confession is made in the moment of the execution, when the punishment is no longer approaching but is already upon them. The sūrah's teaching is that the confession at the point of execution is not the reversal of the choice. The choice was made in the years before, in the spending (v. 9), in the play (v. 2), in the diverted heart (v. 3). The confession is the sound of the body's recognition, and the recognition is the last word, and the last word is the stillness (v. 15).

The Qur'an returns to this position in several verses, and the repetition is the sūrah's way of stating that the position is the standing order. The closest parallel is 47:9–10, in the context of the day of battle: *"And [it is not] as though the disbeliever, when he has come to the extremity of death and God and the angels are extending their hands, [saying]: 'Bring forth your souls! Today you will be paid the penalty of humiliation for what you used to say against God other than the truth...'"* — the confession at the extremity of death, and the sūrah's answer: it is not accepted. The parallel is 39:54–55: *"No soul today will be saved except by His mercy — whether He hastens for you or delays. They say: If we had but listened or used reason, we would not be among the companions of the blaze. So they admitted their sin; so may the blaze of Hell do with them."* The pattern is constant: the confession at the point of the end is not the reversal of the choice, and the choice is the work of the years before.

**The Psychological Lesson: The Difference Between Fear and Turning**

The sūrah's teaching for the reader is the distinction between the recognition that comes from fear and the recognition that comes from turning. The confession of verse 14 is a recognition that comes from fear — the fear of the thing that is coming, the fear of the taunt, the fear of the stillness. The recognition that comes from fear is not the reversal of the choice, because the fear is the answer of the body to the thing that is coming, and the answer of the body is not the choice. The recognition that comes from turning is the recognition that is made in the years before, in the quiet of the heart, in the moment when the reminder is heard (v. 2) and the heart turns toward it instead of playing. The sūrah's teaching is that the two recognitions are not the same, and that the difference between them is the difference between the cry of verse 14 and the silence of the heart that has turned.

The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the spending, made in the years before the arrival. The reader is meant to examine his own life against the pattern: is the recognition being made in the years before the contact, or is it being made at the point of contact? The sūrah's answer is in the sequence: the recognition in the years before is the work of the heart, and the heart is the seat of the choice. The verse, in a single line, is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the confession at the point of contact is too late, and the too-lateness is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the recognition must be made in the years before.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:15

> **And that did not cease to be their cry, till We made them a mown field, stilled.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Cry That Increases Nothing but Flight**

The fifteenth verse closes the sūrah's dramatic sequence, and it closes it by describing what the confession accomplished: nothing. The structure of the verse, as the source renders it, is a continuation that ends in a stillness — "And that did not cease to be their cry, till We made them a mown field, stilled." The cry of verse 14 — *"Oh, woe unto us! Truly we have been wrongdoers"* — does not stop; it continues, it is repeated, it is the sound that fills the space between the recognition and the end. And the sūrah's accounting of the cry is devastating in its precision: the cry did not reverse the flight, it did not open a door, it did not move a hand. It simply continued — and while it continued, the end arrived.

This is the sūrah's final word on the confession, and it is a word the reader should not soften. The confession was true — "we were wrongdoers" is a correct description of the people, and the sūrah never disputes the words. What the sūrah denies is that the words, spoken at the point of impact, were an act. An act is a word that changes a course; a cry at the point of impact is a sound that accompanies a course already fixed. The distinction is the distinction between the person who says "I was wrong" in the years before and changes, and the person who says it in the moment of the blow and continues. The sūrah's sequence has already shown the difference (v. 12's flight was the body's last word before the cry; the cry came after, not before). The cry is the sound the sequence makes as it closes.

**"A Mown Field" — The Image of the Harvested Land**

The image of the end is one of the most vivid in the sūrah: the people are made into a mown field. The image belongs to the language of the harvest — the root ḥ-ṣ-d, to reap, to cut down the standing grain. A mown field is not a field that has died slowly; it is a field that was standing full, green, and heavy with the season, and was cut in a single pass. The sūrah's choice of the image is precise, because the towns described in verse 11 were, at the point of the shattering, exactly that: standing, full, enjoying their *naʿīm* (v. 13), heavy with the season of their luxury — and then cut in a single pass.

The image also carries the silence of the end. A mown field is a field without birdsong, without the rustle of the standing grain, without the insects that live in it. The harvest does not make a lasting noise; it leaves a flat, quiet surface where the standing crop was. This is why the sūrah's image of the end of the wicked is not the image of a battlefield but the image of a harvested field: the end of the *ẓālimūn* is not a drama; it is a silence. The Qur'an elsewhere uses the same family of images for the destroyed nations — the land found dry and barren, the houses with their tops in them (20:40–41, in the account of the men of Mūsā who passed the destroyed people and asked, *"Who can restore us to life after our death?"*) — and the family of images is constant: the end is the stillness, and the stillness is the mark of the end.

**"Stilled" — The Extinguishing and the Word Khāmid**

The final word of the verse — the word the source identifies as *khāmidīn*, "stilled" — is the word of the extinguished flame. *Khāmid* is the fire that has gone out: not the fire that is covered, not the fire that is dampened, but the fire that has consumed its fuel and is no longer a fire. The sūrah is using the word to describe the end of the people as an extinguishing — the flame of the life has consumed its fuel, which was the *naʿīm* they preferred to the reckoning (v. 13), and the flame is out.

The word is doing a piece of theology the sūrah has been building toward. The life of the heedless, in the sūrah's own vocabulary, is a fire — it burns bright, it consumes, it makes noise (the cry of v. 14, the play of v. 2, the mockery of v. 2, the secret councils of v. 3). But a fire that consumes its own fuel is a fire that is already extinguishing itself. The *khāmid* is not a state that is done to the fire from outside; it is the state the fire arrives at when it has spent everything it had. The sūrah's use of the word is therefore not a description of a punishment inflicted; it is a description of a spending completed. The towns are *khāmidīn* for the same reason the prodigal of verse 9 were *mubḍīʿīn*: the spending is finished, and the end is the arithmetic of the spending made visible. The sūrah's justice, stated this way, is not vengeance; it is the audit.

**The Sequence Completed: What the Sūrah Has Just Shown**

The verse's placement at the end of the sūrah's dramatic sequence is the final teaching. The sūrah has now shown the full arc in five verses: the pattern (v. 11), the sight and the flight (v. 12), the taunt (v. 13), the confession (v. 14), and the stillness (v. 15). And the arc is not a story told for information; it is a mechanism shown for recognition. The sūrah is telling the reader that the mechanism is the standing order of the world — the same order that ran in the towns of the past, and the same order that will run in the town of the present if the spending continues.

The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout, now stated in the language of the image: a life is a field. The question is not whether the field will be cut — every field is cut; that is what fields are for. The question is whether the field will have been standing full at the point of the cutting — heavy with grain that can be gathered — or whether it will have been spent on the *naʿīm* that the cutting finds and finds nothing. The sūrah's sequence is the harvest, and the harvest does not negotiate. The reader who has been auditing his own spending (vv. 9, 13) now has the image to audit it with: is your field standing, or is it already mown? The sūrah does not answer the question; the field answers it, in the season of its growth, long before the cutter arrives.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:16

> **And We did not create Heaven and earth and whatsoever is between them in play.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Turn from History to Creation: The Sūrah's Third Movement**

The sixteenth verse opens the sūrah's third movement, and the turn it makes is one of the largest in the sūrah. The sūrah has just closed its second movement with the stillness of the mown field (v. 15) — the end of the towns, the silence after the harvest. And now it opens the third movement with a statement about the creation itself: *"And We did not create Heaven and earth and whatsoever is between them in play."* The sūrah is no longer telling the reader what happened to other people; it is telling the reader what is the case about the world the reader is standing in. The mode changes from narrative to argument, and the argument is the argument the whole sūrah has been preparing for: the world is not a play, and the life that treats it as a play is the life the sūrah has just shown ending in the mown field.

The connection is deliberate, and the reader should not skip it. The word "play" in this verse is the same word the sūrah has been using for the heedless since verse 2 — the play in which the Reminder was received, the play that became the luxury of verse 13, the play that ended in the stillness of verse 15. The sūrah is now stating the contrast at its widest: the Creator did not make the world in play, and the heedless make their lives in play, and the end of the play is the end the sūrah has just shown. The argument of the third movement is therefore not a digression from the sūrah's warning; it is the warning's foundation. A warning about the end of the world only has weight if the world is a thing that has an end — and a world that was created in play is a world that has no end, no account, no reckoning. The sūrah's first verse promised a reckoning (v. 1); verse 16 is the proof that the reckoning is possible, because the world is not play.

**"In Play" — The Denial and Its Theological Weight**

The word *laʿib* — "play" — is not a light word in the Qur'anic usage, and the sūrah's denial of it for the creation is one of the most repeated statements in the Book. The parallel verses are numerous and consistent: *"We did not create the heaven and the earth and that which is between them in play"* (21:16); *"We did not create the heaven and the earth and that which is between them in vain"* (21:16 in its family, and 38:27: *"We did not create the heaven and the earth and that which is between them in vain"*); *"Do you think that We have created you in play and that you will not be brought back to Us?"* (23:115). The repetition across the sūrahs is the Qur'an's way of stating that the denial is a foundation, not a detail — that the whole argument of the Book stands or falls on it.

The denial works on two levels. The first level is cosmological: the creation is purposeful. The heavens and the earth, and everything between them, were made for a reason — the reason the next verse will develop ("Had We desired... We would surely have taken it from that which was with Us," v. 17). A cosmos that is purposeful is a cosmos that is intelligible, that can be read, that can be addressed, and that can be judged. The second level is moral: the word "play" is the word the sūrah has been using for the heedless, and the denial is therefore also a command. The sūrah is saying, in the language of the creation: you are not a play, and do not live as one. The person who lives his life in play is living against the nature of the world he lives in — the way a ship built to sail is wronged if it is left to rot in a dry dock. The sūrah's denial of the play is both a statement about God (He did not create in play) and a statement about the human being (you were not made for play), and the two statements are the same statement, because the human being is the part of the creation that was made to know its purpose.

**The Logic of the Argument: A Cosmos That Is Not Play Is a Cosmos That Is Addressed**

The logical structure of the verse is worth stating plainly, because it is the structure the sūrah will build on for the next thirteen verses. A thing that is play is a thing that has no purpose, no audience, no end. A thing that is not play is a thing that has a purpose, an audience, and an end. The sūrah's denial of the play is therefore the affirmation of three things at once: the purpose (the will of the Creator, which verse 17 will state), the audience (the human being, to whom the creation is addressed — the whole sūrah is that address), and the end (the reckoning, which verse 1 promised). The three affirmations are not separate claims; they are the three faces of the single claim that the world is not play.

The modern echo of the argument is the contrast between the two universes the modern mind is offered. The first universe is the play: a cosmos with no purpose, no audience, no end — a cosmos in which the death of a star and the death of a person are the same event, and in which the moral life is a private preference with no cosmic weight. The second universe is the sūrah's: a cosmos with a purpose, an audience, and an end — a cosmos in which the moral life is the one thing that the cosmos was, in a sense, made for, because the moral life is the life that knows the purpose. The sūrah's argument is not that the second universe is more comfortable; it is that the second universe is the only one in which the first verse's promise (the reckoning) makes sense. A play cannot be audited; a purpose can. The sūrah's first verse promised an audit; verse 16 is the proof that the audit is possible.

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Foundation: What the Third Movement Will Build**

The verse's placement at the beginning of the sūrah's third movement is the final teaching. The movement that follows — verses 16 to 29 — will build on this foundation in a precise order: the denial of the play (v. 16), the statement of the purpose (v. 17), the victory of the truth over the falsehood (v. 18), the submission of the angels (vv. 19–20), the refutation of the earthly gods (v. 21), the proof of the Oneness of God by the argument of the two gods (v. 22), the unanswerability of God and the answerability of the creatures (v. 23), the demand for the proof (v. 24), the one message of all the messengers (v. 25), and the refutation of the angels as daughters of God (vv. 26–29). Each of these verses is a building on the foundation of verse 16: the world is not play, and therefore it has a purpose, and therefore the purpose has a truth, and therefore the truth has a form (the Oneness of God), and therefore the form has a servant (the angel who glorifies), and therefore the servant has a master (the One who is not questioned), and therefore the master has a message (the one message of all the messengers), and therefore the message has a refutation (the refutation of the daughters of God). The sūrah's third movement is a single argument in thirteen verses, and verse 16 is the first premise of the argument.

The reader who understands verse 16 as a foundation, and not as a detail, will read the rest of the movement as the argument it is — and will see that the sūrah's warning (the first two movements) and the sūrah's proof (the third movement) are not two separate things but one: the warning is the proof applied to the reader, and the proof is the warning made explicit. The sūrah's method is therefore not a method of information; it is a method of address, and the address is the work of the sūrah. The world is not play, and the reader is the audience, and the reckoning is the end — and the sūrah's question, from the first verse to the last, is the same question: will the reader live as the audience, or will the reader live as the play?

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:17

> **Had We desired to take up a diversion, We would surely have taken it from that which was with Us, were We to do so.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Counterfactual That Proves the Needlessness**

The seventeenth verse is one of the most subtle arguments in the sūrah, and its subtlety is in its form: it is a counterfactual, a statement about what would have happened had God willed something that He did not will. The structure is: "Had We desired to take up a diversion, We would surely have taken it from that which was with Us." The sūrah is not describing an event; it is describing a possibility that was never actualized, and the point of the description is to show that the possibility proves the needlessness. The argument runs: if the creation were God's play, or His diversion, or His amusement, then God would have had a need — a need for entertainment, for a companion, for a child, for a pastime. But the verse says: had such a need existed, God would have taken the diversion "from that which was with Us" — from Himself, from His own essence, from His own fullness. He would not have needed to create the world to amuse Himself. The world, in other words, is not God's diversion; the world is God's purpose, and the purpose is not a need.

The source records the commentators' readings of the word "diversion" (*laʿb*), and the range is instructive. Some understand it to mean "consort" or "wife" — the attribution of a partner to God, the claim that God is married, that God has a spouse. Others understand it to mean "child" — the claim that God has a son, that God is a father in the biological sense. Both readings point to the same theological error: the attribution to God of a need that belongs only to the finite. A finite being needs a companion because he is alone; a finite being needs a child because he is mortal. God needs neither, because He is neither alone nor mortal. The sūrah's counterfactual is therefore a refutation of the attribution: had God needed a diversion, a consort, or a child, He would have taken it from Himself, and He would not have needed the world. The world is not God's need; the world is God's will.

**"From That Which Was with Us" — The Divine Self-Sufficiency**

The phrase *min ʿindinā* — "from that which was with Us" — is the theological heart of the verse, and it states one of the most fundamental attributes of God in the Qur'anic account: His self-sufficiency, His *ghinan*. God is not a being who lacks something; He is the being who lacks nothing. The world is not the answer to a divine need; the world is the expression of a divine will. The distinction is the distinction between the artist who creates because he is bored, and the artist who creates because he is full. The first artist's creation is his diversion; the second artist's creation is his gift. The sūrah's counterfactual is the sūrah's way of stating that God is the second artist, not the first: He creates from fullness, not from need.

The Prophetic confirmation of this attribute is the hadith qudsī that the tradition preserves in its most explicit form:

> *"O children of Adam, I have sent down to you a writ, and I have made a writ against Myself: I am Self-Sufficient (al-Ghanī), and I hate the self-sufficient (ghaniyy) among My servants."* (al-Bukhārī; Muslim)

The hadith's logic is the sūrah's logic: God's self-sufficiency is not a private attribute; it is the basis of His relation to the creature. The creature is not the answer to God's need; the creature is the object of God's will. And the creature's sin is the sin of self-sufficiency — the creature who acts as though he is God, who acts as though he lacks nothing, who acts as though the world is his to command. The sūrah's counterfactual is the sūrah's way of stating that the creature's self-sufficiency is the creature's error, and that God's self-sufficiency is God's nature.

**The Transcendence of God: The Refutation of the Commingling**

The verse's deeper teaching is the transcendence of God — the refutation of the "commingling" that the source notes some commentators drew from the verse. The attribution of a consort or a child to God is, in the end, an attribution of a commingling: the claim that God is mixed with the created, that God is entangled with the finite, that God is a being who needs the other. The sūrah's counterfactual refutes the commingling: had God needed the other, He would have taken the other from Himself, and He would not have needed the world. The world is not the other that God needs; the world is the other that God wills. The distinction is the distinction between need and will, and the sūrah's argument is that God's relation to the world is a relation of will, not of need.

This is the sūrah's answer to the pantheist reading of the creation — the reading that identifies God with the world, that makes the world God's body, that treats the cosmos as the divine self. The sūrah's counterfactual is the refutation of the pantheist: God is not the world; God is the One who wills the world. The world is not God's play; the world is God's purpose. And the purpose is not a need; the purpose is a will. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that God is far from the world; it is that God is not mixed with the world, and that the relation of God to the world is the relation of the willer to the willed, not the relation of the needy to the needed.

**The Modern Echo: The Universe as Experiment and the Universe as Purpose**

The verse's teaching for the modern reader is the one the sūrah's context makes plain. The modern mind is offered two accounts of the cosmos. The first account is the account of the play: the cosmos is an accident, a random process, a universe with no purpose, no audience, no end — a universe in which the death of a star and the death of a person are the same event. The second account is the sūrah's: the cosmos is not a play; the cosmos is a purpose, and the purpose is the will of the One who wills it. The sūrah's counterfactual is the sūrah's way of stating that the first account is the account of the needy — the account of the being who creates because he is bored, who creates because he is alone, who creates because he is mortal. The second account is the account of the self-sufficient — the account of the being who creates from fullness, who wills from purpose, who addresses the world as the audience.

The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the orientation. The reader is meant to examine his own account of the cosmos: is the cosmos a play, or is the cosmos a purpose? The sūrah's teaching is that the answer to that question is the orientation of the heart, and the orientation of the heart is the work of the choice. The verse, in a single line, is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that God is not the needy; God is the self-sufficient, and the self-sufficient is the One who wills the world as a purpose, not as a play.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:18

> **Nay, but We cast truth against falsehood, and it crushes it, and, behold, it vanishes. And woe unto you for that which you describe!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Cast Truth Against Falsehood" — The Physics of the Encounter**

The eighteenth verse is one of the most powerful single statements in the sūrah, and its power is in its image. The sūrah does not say that the truth "defeats" the falsehood, or that the truth "overcomes" the falsehood, or that the truth "exposes" the falsehood. It says that the truth is *cast against* the falsehood — the image is of a heavy object thrown at a lighter one, of a stone hurled at a wall of sand. The verb *damagha* — "it crushes it" — is the word of the skull, the word of the blow that reaches the brain, the word of the stamp that impresses its form into the softer material. The sūrah's image is therefore not a description of a debate; it is a description of a collision, and the collision is the physics of the encounter between the truth and the falsehood.

The image is one of the most repeated in the Qur'an, and its repetition is the sūrah's way of stating that the physics is the standing order. The parallel verses are numerous: *"Truth has come, and falsehood has departed; indeed, falsehood is, in the end, bound to depart"* (17:81); *"So that He may verify the truth and prove falsehood to be false"* (8:8); *"Truth has come, and falsehood originates naught, nor restores"* (34:49); *"God wipes out falsehood and verifies the truth through His Words"* (42:24). The family of verses is constant: the truth is cast, the falsehood is crushed, and the falsehood vanishes. The sūrah's use of the image in this verse is the return of the image, and the return is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the physics is the standing order.

**"It Crushes It" — The Word Damagha and Its Weight**

The word *damagha* — "it crushes it" — is the word of the skull. The root d-m-g-h is the root of *dimāgh*, the brain, and the verb *damagha* means to strike the skull so as to reach the brain, to crush the head, to break the resistance of the harder part. The sūrah is using the word to describe the encounter between the truth and the falsehood not as a debate but as a collision, and the collision is the physics of the encounter. The truth is not the louder voice; the truth is the heavier object. The falsehood is not the weaker argument; the falsehood is the softer material. The sūrah's image is therefore not a description of a rhetorical victory; it is a description of a physical event, and the physical event is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the truth is the heavier, and the falsehood is the softer, and the heavier crushes the softer.

The word also carries the sense of the stamp — the object that impresses its form into the softer material, the seal that leaves its mark on the wax. The sūrah's use of the word in this sense is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the truth does not merely defeat the falsehood; the truth impresses its form into the falsehood, and the form of the truth is the form that remains. The falsehood is not erased; the falsehood is over-stamped, and the stamp of the truth is the mark that is left. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the truth is the victory; it is that the truth is the form, and the form is the mark that remains after the collision.

**"Behold, It Vanishes" — The Fate of the Falsehood**

The final clause of the first half of the verse — *fa-innaahu khāʾibun* — "and, behold, it vanishes" — is the sūrah's statement of the fate of the falsehood. The word *khāʾib* — "vanishing," "disappointed," "frustrated" — is the word of the one who returns empty-handed, the one who sought and found nothing, the one who built and found his building gone. The sūrah is using the word to describe the end of the falsehood not as a destruction but as a vanishing — the falsehood is not destroyed by a force from outside; the falsehood vanishes because it has nothing in it, because it is the absence of the truth, and the absence vanishes when the presence is there.

The sūrah's use of the word *khāʾib* is therefore not a description of a physical end; it is a description of the metaphysical end. The falsehood is not a thing that is destroyed; the falsehood is an absence that is filled. The truth is cast, and the absence is filled, and the filling is the vanishing of the falsehood. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the truth is the destroyer; it is that the truth is the filler, and the filling is the vanishing of the falsehood. The reader is meant to hear the verse as a description of the metaphysics of the encounter, and the metaphysics is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the falsehood is not a rival of the truth; the falsehood is the absence of the truth, and the absence vanishes when the presence is there.

**"Woe Unto You for That Which You Describe" — The Curse and the Ascription**

The second half of the verse — *wa-waylan lakum mimmā tawsifūn* — "And woe unto you for that which you describe" — is the sūrah's curse, and the curse is directed at the ascription. The source notes that the phrase can refer to the lies the wrongdoers told, or to their idolatry, recalling the phrases such as *"Exalted is God above the partners they ascribe"* (7:190; 23:92; 27:63), which exalt God above idolatrous attributions. The sūrah's curse is therefore not directed at a single error; it is directed at the pattern of the ascription — the habit of describing God in the terms of the finite, of attributing to God the needs of the creature, of making the world the body of God, of treating the created as the uncreated.

The word *wayl* — "woe" — is the word of the cry, the word of the body's answer to the thing that is coming. The sūrah's use of the word here is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the ascription is not a minor error; the ascription is the thing that brings the woe, and the woe is the end of the ascription. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the ascription is a mistake; it is that the ascription is the choice, and the choice is the work of the heart, and the heart is the seat of the choice. The verse, in a single line, is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the truth is cast, the falsehood is crushed, and the woe is the end of the ascription.

**The Logic of the Argument: Why the Truth Crushes the Falsehood**

The sūrah's argument is worth stating plainly, because it is the argument the sūrah will build on in the rest of the third movement. The logic is this. The truth is a presence; the falsehood is an absence. A presence, when it is cast against an absence, fills the absence, and the filling is the vanishing of the absence. The truth is therefore not a rival of the falsehood; the truth is the answer to the falsehood, and the answer is the filling of the absence. The sūrah's image of the collision is the sūrah's way of stating that the truth is the heavier, the falsehood is the softer, and the heavier crushes the softer. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the truth is the victory; it is that the truth is the presence, and the presence is the answer to the absence, and the answer is the filling.

The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the ascription. The reader is meant to examine his own descriptions of God, of the world, of himself: are the descriptions the descriptions of the presence, or are the descriptions the descriptions of the absence? The sūrah's teaching is that the descriptions of the absence are the ascriptions, and the ascriptions are the choice, and the choice is the work of the heart. The verse, in a single line, is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the truth is cast, the falsehood is crushed, and the woe is the end of the ascription.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:19

> **Unto Him belongs whosoever is in the heavens and on earth. Those who are with Him are not too arrogant to worship Him; nor do they weary.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Unto Him Belongs Whosoever" — The Statement of Ownership**

The nineteenth verse opens the sūrah's account of the angels with a statement that is the foundation of the account: *"Unto Him belongs whosoever is in the heavens and on earth."* The sūrah is not describing a part of the cosmos; it is describing the whole cosmos, and the description is the description of ownership. The heavens and the earth, and everything in them, are not the property of the heavens and the earth; they are the property of the One who owns them. The sūrah's statement is therefore not a cosmological statement; it is a statement of the relation between the Creator and the created, and the relation is the relation of ownership.

The statement is one of the most repeated in the Qur'an, and its repetition is the sūrah's way of stating that the ownership is the standing order. The parallel verses are numerous: *"To Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth"* (2:284, in its wider context); *"To Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and on the earth, and to Him is the religion, entirely, in the first instance and the last"* (7:183). The family of verses is constant: the ownership is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the cosmos is not self-owning; the cosmos is owned, and the owner is the One who owns it.

**"Those Who Are with Him" — The Angels and the Nearness**

The second half of the verse introduces the angels: *"Those who are with Him are not too arrogant to worship Him; nor do they weary."* The phrase *ʿindahu* — "with Him" — is one of the most discussed phrases in the sūrah, and the discussion is the discussion of the nearness. The angels are "with Him" — near Him, in His presence, in the court of the King. The nearness is not a spatial nearness; it is a nearness of rank, a nearness of honor, a nearness of service. The angels are not in a place next to God; the angels are in the rank that is the highest rank of the created, and the rank is the nearness.

The sūrah's use of the phrase *ʿindahu* is therefore not a description of a location; it is a description of a status. The angels are the ones who are "with Him" in the sense that they are the ones who are in the highest rank of the created, and the rank is the nearness. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the angels are in a place; it is that the angels are in a rank, and the rank is the nearness, and the nearness is the honor. The reader is meant to hear the verse as a description of the status of the angels, and the status is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the nearness is the honor, and the honor is the rank.

**"Not Too Arrogant to Worship Him" — The Humility of the Highest**

The phrase *lā yastaḍʿinūna ʿalayhi* — "not too arrogant to worship Him" — is the verse's most important line, and it is the line that states the sūrah's teaching on the relation between rank and service. The sūrah is saying that the angels, the highest of the created, the ones who are "with Him" in the highest rank, are not too arrogant to worship. The rank does not diminish the service; the honor does not diminish the humility. The angels are the ones who are the furthest from arrogance, because they are the ones who know their rank, and the knowledge of the rank is the knowledge of the humility.

The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the angels are humble; it is that the angels are the ones who know their rank, and the knowledge of the rank is the knowledge of the humility. The contrast is with the human being who is too important to serve, who is too honored to worship, who is too arrogant to bow. The sūrah's use of the phrase is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the arrogance is the human error, and the humility is the angelic nature, and the human being who is arrogant is the human being who has forgotten his rank, and the human being who has forgotten his rank is the human being who is too important to serve.

The image is one of the most vivid in the sūrah: the highest beings in the cosmos are the most humble before God. The king's cupbearer is not too important to serve the king; the angel is not too honored to worship God. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the angels are the model of the human being; it is that the angels are the model of the humility, and the humility is the model of the human being who knows his rank.

**"Nor Do They Weary" — The Continuity of the Service**

The final phrase of the verse — *wa-lā yaʿbudūn* (in the sense of "nor do they weary") — is the sūrah's statement of the continuity of the angelic service. The angels do not tire of the worship; the worship is not a burden to them; the worship is their nature. The sūrah's use of the word is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the angelic service is not a task that is performed out of duty; the angelic service is a state that is lived out of nature. The angels do not pray because they must; the angels pray because they are, and the being is the praying.

The source records the commentators' reading that for the angels the glorification of God is what breathing is for human beings — the reading of al-Ṭabarī and several others. The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the angelic worship is not a separate act; the angelic worship is the angel's way of being in the world, and the way of being is the worship. The parallel for the human being is the human being whose worship is not a separate act; the human being whose worship is the human being's way of being in the world, and the way of being is the worship. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the angels are the model of the prayer; it is that the angels are the model of the state, and the state is the model of the human being who is at rest in the worship.

The sūrah's use of the phrase *wa-lā yaʿbudūn* is therefore not a description of a physical end; it is a description of the continuity of the service, and the continuity is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the angelic service is the service that does not end, and the service that does not end is the service that is the nature. The reader is meant to hear the verse as a description of the angelic state, and the state is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the humility is the rank, and the rank is the nearness, and the nearness is the honor, and the honor is the service that does not end.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:20

> **They glorify night and day, without tiring.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Glorification Without Interval**

Verse 19 established that the beings with God are neither arrogant nor weary; verse 20 states what they do in place of both. *Yusabbiḥūna al-layla wa-l-nahāra lā yafturūn* — "They glorify night and day, without tiring." The pairing of night and day is not decorative. It is the Qur'an's standing idiom for unbroken duration, and it forecloses at the outset the assumption that worship must be interrupted by sleep, by boredom, or by the pull of competing concerns. Human worship is necessarily punctuated; the verse describes a worship that is not, and it does so in order to establish a measure against which the worship described four verses later can be assessed.

The verb *yusabbiḥūna* is the intensive form, and its sense is not merely that they praise but that they declare God free of deficiency. What the angels are doing, on the classical reading, is not accumulating merit through repetition but continuously asserting the transcendence of the One they worship. That distinction matters for what follows, because the sūrah is about to contrast this worship with the worship of stones, and the contrast is not between more and less of the same operation but between two entirely different kinds of thing — one an act of recognition, the other an act of projection.

Al-Ṭabarī preserves an observation that gives the image its force: for the angels, the glorification of God is what breathing is for human beings. Breathing is not an act one performs between other acts; it is the condition of performing any act at all. The analogy therefore does not say that the angels praise God frequently or even constantly in the sense of a long sequence of discrete acts. It says that praise is their mode of existence, as involuntary and as sustaining as respiration, and that to cease would not be to stop an activity but to stop being what they are. Worship, for them, is not something they do.

**Why the Angels Cannot Weary**

Al-Rāzī draws out the theological consequence that al-Ṭabarī left implicit. Not tiring and not becoming bored is possible only for angels, not for human beings. The distinction is worth pausing on because it prevents the verse from becoming an impossible demand addressed to the reader. Human worship is intermittent by constitution, and the Qur'an nowhere asks it to be otherwise; what it asks is that the intervals be filled with something other than a rival object of worship. The verse describes an ideal that is constitutively unavailable to human beings precisely so that no human being will be measured against it.

There is a second implication, and it concerns the object rather than the worshipper. Fatigue in worship arises when the object is limited — when one has exhausted what there is to say about it, or when the saying ceases to disclose anything new. The angels' indefatigable praise is thus evidence about its object rather than about them: they do not tire because there is no bottom to what they are praising. A finite object would produce boredom in an immortal worshipper within a finite time, and the absence of boredom is therefore a report about infinity rather than about angelic stamina.

It is also worth noticing that al-Ṭabarī glosses *nor do they weary* as meaning that they neither tire nor become bored. The two are different failures. Tiring is a failure of capacity; boredom is a failure of interest. Human worship is vulnerable to both, and the second is the more insidious because it can persist in a body that is not exhausted — a person may continue the forms of worship long after they have ceased to disclose anything. By naming both and denying both of the angels, the verse identifies worship's two characteristic decay modes and locates their remedy in the inexhaustibility of the object.

**The Contrast the Verse Is Building**

The sūrah has been assembling an argument, and verse 20 is the final piece before it turns. Verses 19–20 describe worship that is continuous, ungrudging, and untiring. Verse 21 then asks a single question — *Or have they taken gods from the earth who resurrect?* — and the force of that question depends entirely on what has just been said. The beings nearest to God worship without ceasing; the gods taken from the earth cannot even give life. The commentators connect this to 41:38, where the same refusal of arrogance and weariness is attributed to those near God, and the recurrence across sūrahs is not accidental.

The rhetorical movement is deliberate and worth attending to, because the sūrah does not begin by attacking the idols. It first establishes the standard of worship among the highest created beings, so that the worship of stones appears not merely false but beneath the dignity of the worshipper. Verse 20 supplies the measure against which verse 21's question is asked, and without it the question would be a bare assertion. An idol is precisely the kind of object that produces boredom, because there is nothing in it to keep disclosing, and the verse has just described a worship in which boredom is impossible.

This ordering is characteristic of the sūrah's method throughout. It establishes what is true before it names what is false, and it does so by appeal to what the audience already accepts rather than to what they must be persuaded of. The Meccan audience did not dispute that the angels worship God; the verse takes that as common ground and then asks what follows from it. The argument is therefore not an appeal to authority but an inference from a premise the audience has already granted, which is why the sūrah can afford to state it in a single clause.

---

**The Verse as a Standard for Human Worship**

If the angels' worship is constitutively unavailable to human beings, the verse still functions as a standard, and its function is to fix the direction rather than the distance. The sūrah does not ask the reader to glorify without interval; it asks the reader to notice what worship looks like when it is not compromised by fatigue, resentment, or distraction, and then to ask what in his own worship is owed to those three things rather than to its object. The measure is set high precisely so that it cannot be mistaken for an achievable target, and so that falling short of it is not experienced as failure but as the ordinary condition of a creature.

There is a further use to which the classical commentators put the verse. Because the angels' glorification is described as their breathing, the verse has been read as an account of what remembrance becomes when it ceases to be an exercise and becomes a disposition. The Sufi exegetes whom the commentators record elsewhere in this sūrah treat that transition as the aim of practice rather than its reward, and the verse supplies the image of what the aim looks like — not a longer list of acts but a different relation to the One who is remembered.

Read this way the verse is not primarily cosmological. It is describing a mode of existence in order to make a point about worship, and the point is that worship's quality is determined by its object's inexhaustibility rather than by the worshipper's effort. A person who tires has not necessarily been inattentive; he may simply have been attending to something finite. The remedy the verse implies is not more discipline but a larger object, which is why the sūrah turns immediately to the question of what has been taken as an object in God's place.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:21

> **Or have they taken gods from the earth who resurrect?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Single Question**

*Am ittakhadhū ālihatan mina'l-arḍi hum yunshirūn.* The verse is a question, not an assertion, and its brevity is the point. After nineteen verses establishing God's ownership of the heavens and the earth and describing the ceaseless worship of the angels, the sūrah compresses its whole critique of idolatry into one clause: have they taken gods *from the earth* who *resurrect*? Two words carry the argument. *From the earth* fixes the origin of these gods — they are not descendents from the heavens, not even fallen ones, but products of the ground, quarried and carved by the people who then worship them.

*Who resurrect* fixes the test — the one function that a god must be able to perform and that no idol has ever been observed to perform. The verse does not need to state the conclusion because the two words already contain it. The interrogative *am* introduces a question that expects a negative answer, and this is a standard feature of Qur'anic rhetoric. It does something a flat assertion could not: it places the burden of response on the hearer. The verse does not say *your gods cannot resurrect*; it asks whether they can, and leaves the audience to supply the answer they already know but have not spoken.

There is also an implicit irony in the phrase *from the earth*. The idols were made of earth by human hands, and the verse asks whether things made from the ground can restore life to the ground. The materials of the idol and the materials of the corpse are the same. Whatever agency is required to reverse death is therefore not resident in the stone, and the verse's choice of vocabulary makes that visible without needing to argue it. The audience is being asked to notice something about the physical composition of the objects in front of them.

**The Argument al-Qurṭubī Draws Out**

Al-Qurṭubī's note is economical: rather than worship the Creator of the heavens and the earth, such people prefer to worship earthly beings incapable of giving life. The word *prefer* is important, because it identifies the choice as a preference rather than a conclusion. Nobody arrives at the worship of a stone by reasoning; people arrive at it by inheritance, by social pressure, or by the convenience of a deity that makes no demands. The verse's diagnosis of idolatry is therefore not that it is a mistaken inference but that it is not an inference at all — it is a habit that has never been examined.

The verse's choice of *resurrect* rather than some lesser function is a deliberate selection of the hardest case. An idol can be credited with many things by generous observers — presence, blessing, intercession, ancestral favour. It cannot be credited with raising the dead. The sūrah therefore does not argue about the marginal claims, where reasonable people might disagree about evidence; it goes straight to the one that decides the matter, and on which no evidence has ever been offered by anyone in any generation. The selection of the test is itself part of the argument.

This method has a consequence worth noting. By choosing the hardest case, the verse makes the lesser claims irrelevant. If the gods in question cannot perform the one function that defines divinity, then whatever else they may be credited with does not make them gods. The sūrah does not need to disprove intercession or blessing or presence, because none of those, even if granted, would establish the thing being claimed. The argument is efficient because it targets the load-bearing claim rather than the decorative ones.

**Resurrection as the Qur'an's Recurring Test**
