The source commentary notes that biological life renewed is treated throughout the Qur'an as a symbol of the Resurrection, and cites 35:9: God sends the winds, they raise clouds, the clouds are driven to a dead land, and the earth is revived after its death — *thus shall be the Resurrection*. The argument is not that the two events are alike in mechanism but that both display the same impossible transition: from nothing to something, from death to life. What makes the annual revival probative is not its similarity to resurrection but its demonstration that the transition is possible at all.

This is why the question is phrased as it is. The Meccan audience already accepted that the earth is revived annually; the observation required no persuasion. The verse asks them to apply an observation they already grant to the deity they have chosen. If the revival of a dead land requires the Creator of the heavens and the earth, then a god taken from the earth cannot be the one performing it. The sūrah returns to this argument at verse 30, where the rending asunder of the heavens and the earth is followed by *And We made every living thing from water*, and then by *Will they not, then, believe?*

The verse presupposes something its audience would not have disputed: that a god is defined by what it can do, not by what it is called. This is worth stating because much of the surrounding polemic assumes the opposite — that divinity is a matter of designation, of ancestral practice, of the name a community has given an object. By asking whether these gods resurrect, the sūrah shifts the entire ground of the debate from designation to capacity. Once that shift is made the outcome is settled, because the capacity in question has never been observed in any idol, and verse 24 makes the shift explicit by demanding *Bring me your proof!*

---

**What the Verse Does Not Argue**

It is worth marking the limits of the verse's claim, because they are as instructive as the claim itself. The verse does not say that the idols do not exist; nobody in the audience supposed that the stones were unreal. It does not say that the ancestors who carved them were foolish, or that the practice is without social function. It asks one question about one capacity, and the question is narrow enough that it cannot be evaded by appealing to tradition, to sentiment, or to the usefulness of the cult.

This narrowness is what makes the verse effective as an argument rather than merely as a rebuke. A broad denunciation invites a broad defence; a single precise question invites a single precise answer, and no answer is available. The commentators' glosses concentrate on exactly this point — that the verse selects the function which defines divinity and asks whether it has ever been observed — and their concentration indicates that they understood the verse's force to lie in its specificity rather than in its tone.

The same method appears at verse 24, where the demand is for *burhān*, a demonstration, and at verse 30, where the appeal is to observation rather than to authority. The sūrah's polemic against idolatry is consistently conducted at the level of specific, checkable claims. This is why it can be so brief. A case built on particulars does not need the length that a case built on generalities requires, and the sūrah's treatment of idolatry is among its most compressed arguments for that reason.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:22

> **Were there gods other than God in them, they would surely have been corrupted. So glory be to God, Lord of the Throne, above that which they ascribe.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Logical Argument**

*Law kāna fīhimā ālihatun illa'Llāh la-fasadatā.* "Were there gods other than God in them, they would surely have been corrupted." This is one of a small group of verses that argue the impossibility of a plurality of gods as a matter of logic rather than revelation. The commentators place it alongside 12:39–40, where Joseph asks his fellow prisoners whether diverse lords are better than God the One, the Paramount, and 23:91, which reasons that if there were other gods each would take away what it created and some would overcome others. The argument runs from the observable order of the world to the unity of its source.

The heavens and the earth display a single coherent system: one set of physical regularities, no interruption, no negotiation between competing agencies, no region of the cosmos governed by different rules. A plurality of sovereigns would produce exactly the interruption that is not observed. Al-Rāzī develops this as a demonstration rather than a rhetorical flourish, and 17:42 makes the same point from the opposite direction — if there were gods with Him, they would seek a way unto the Possessor of the Throne. The evidence offered is the persistence of the world, which is available to anyone without prior instruction.

It is worth being precise about what the argument does and does not establish. It does not demonstrate the existence of God from the order of nature; the sūrah takes that as already granted by its audience. It demonstrates the *unity* of the source of that order, on the ground that a plurality of independent sovereigns would produce competition, and competition would produce the disorder that is absent. The verse is therefore an argument within theism rather than an argument for it, which is why it can be made so briefly — it is completing a case rather than opening one.

**The Grammar That Carries the Formula**

The source commentary notes that *illa'Llāh* can be rendered either "other than God" or "except for God," and that the second reading reproduces the second half of *lā ilāha illa'Llāh*. This is not a curiosity. It means the verse's central clause is structurally identical to the declaration of faith, so that the logical argument and the creedal formula are the same sentence in two registers — one stating a conditional about the world, the other stating a commitment. The verse thereby shows that the creed is not an arbitrary formula but a compressed statement of the demonstration just given.

The commentary also notes that *fīhimā* — "in them" — uses a dual pronoun referring to the heavens and the earth. The duality is significant. The verse takes the two most comprehensive domains of created existence and asserts that neither contains a competing god. There is no third domain in which such a god could be located, and no gap in which it could hide. The dual form closes off the evasion available to a polytheist who might grant that the heavens belong to God while reserving the earth, or the reverse.

The conditional *law kāna* is counterfactual, and its counterfactuality is doing real work. The verse does not say that there are other gods who happen to be well behaved; it says that if there were, the heavens and the earth would have collapsed. The observable persistence of the world is therefore offered as the evidence for the conclusion, which is why the argument can be made to an audience that has never read a philosophical text. What is required is not technical training but the willingness to notice that the world has not fallen apart.

**Al-Sulamī's Reading and the Verse's Close**

The note records that al-Sulamī reads the verse as an allegory of the heart: a divided heart will become corrupted, and a heart must be unified and detached from entanglements in the world. On this reading *fa-sadatā* — "they would surely have been corrupted" — describes not a cosmological collapse but the disintegration of an interior life divided between competing objects of ultimate concern. The allegory preserves the logical structure of the verse rather than replacing it: what makes a plurality of gods impossible in the cosmos is what makes a plurality of ultimates destructive in a person.

The practical consequence is not abstract. A person with two ultimate concerns does not experience them as a stable compromise; they experience the conflict between them as corrosion. Al-Sulamī's gloss identifies that corrosion as the same phenomenon the verse identifies in the heavens and the earth, and it thereby makes the verse available as a diagnosis of ordinary interior experience rather than only as a proposition about metaphysics. The commentators do not treat these as two separate meanings but as one truth at two scales, and the spiritual reading depends on the cosmological one for its force.

The verse closes with *fa-subḥāna'Llāhi rabbi'l-ʿarsh ʿammā yaṣifūn* — "So glory be to God, Lord of the Throne, above that which they ascribe." The formula *subḥānahu* is the Qur'an's standard marker that a preceding statement has attributed to God something incompatible with His nature, and its appearance here signals that the plurality just refuted was not a neutral hypothesis but an indignity. The title *Lord of the Throne* completes the argument by naming the sovereignty the hypothetical plurality would have displaced: the gods they ascribe are not additions to God but replacements for Him.

---

**The Argument Read Against Verse 21**

Verses 21 and 22 are a matched pair and lose much of their force when read apart. Verse 21 asks whether gods taken from the earth can resurrect; verse 22 asks what the world would look like if there were gods other than God. The first is a question about capacity, the second about coherence, and together they close off the two routes by which polytheism might be defended — that the gods can do something, and that they can coexist.

The order matters. The capacity question comes first because it is the simpler and because an audience that cannot answer it will not reach the second. Only after the gods have been shown incapable of the defining function does the verse turn to the structural argument, which is more demanding and more general. The sūrah is not presenting two independent proofs but a sequence in which the second builds on the failure of the first.

This sequencing also explains why the verse can move from the conditional to the doxology so abruptly. By the time *la-fasadatā* has been stated, the case is closed, and what remains is not further argument but the recognition that the alternative just refuted would have been an indignity. *Subḥāna'Llāh* is not a conclusion drawn from the argument; it is the response the argument makes appropriate, and the verse places it immediately after the conditional for that reason.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:23

> **He shall not be questioned about what He does, but they shall be questioned.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Asymmetry of Answerability**

*Lā yus'alu ʿammā yafʿalu wa-hum yus'alūn.* "He shall not be questioned about what He does, but they shall be questioned." Al-Rāzī states the point plainly: God is not answerable to creation, but creatures are answerable to Him. The verse does not argue for this asymmetry; it announces it, and the announcement is itself part of the argument, since a being who could be called to account by his creatures would not be sovereign over them. The symmetry of the sentence is exact — the same verb, negated in the first clause and affirmed in the second — and that exactness is the verse's method.

The source commentary notes that the idea of questioning in the sense of responsibility also arises at verse 13 of this sūrah and at 2:119, 2:134, and 16:56. The recurrence matters. The Qur'an returns repeatedly to the theme because it is the point at which divine sovereignty and human moral seriousness meet, and neither can be surrendered without losing the other. A God who must justify Himself is not sovereign; a creature who is never questioned is not moral. The verse holds both halves together in a single balanced sentence precisely because separating them produces one of these two errors.

It is worth noting what the verse does not say. It does not say that God's acts are arbitrary, nor that human questioning is worthless in every context. It says that God will not be questioned about what He does, which is a statement about the direction of accountability rather than about the availability of reasons. The distinction is preserved in the commentators' glosses and is often lost in summary, where the verse is read as forbidding inquiry rather than as describing a relation in which inquiry runs one way.

**Can One Ask God Why?**

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both connect the verse to teleology — whether human beings may ask God why He did such and such. The commentary offers an alternative rendering that sharpens the issue: *He is unanswerable for what He does, but they are answerable*. Under this reading the verse is not forbidding a question but stating a structural fact about the difference between created and uncreated agency. Al-Rāzī identifies the theological stake precisely: with created things one can ask why they acted as they did, because they act from motives and causes that are in principle inspectable.

It is by definition impossible for creatures to know why God does what He does, and what is at stake is whether God stands in need of a reason outside Himself in order to act. If He did, that reason would be prior to Him and would be the true sovereign. The verse's asymmetry is therefore a consequence of divine unity rather than an assertion of arbitrary power — the same unity argued in the preceding verse from the coherence of the cosmos. This connection is easy to miss because the two verses look like different topics, one about the structure of the world and one about accountability.

They are the same argument. A single unconditioned source is what makes the world coherent and what makes creatures answerable rather than the reverse. Al-Qurṭubī records that the issue of free will versus predestination arises here, in that human beings cannot, on a strictly predestinarian reading, question God or ask why one was made a disbeliever in life. The commentary links this to *God provides for whomsoever He will without reckoning*, understood by some to mean that no one can bring God to account for the provisions He gives or withholds. The verse does not resolve the tension, and the commentators do not pretend that it does.

**"But They Shall Be Questioned"**

The second half of the verse receives less attention than the first and deserves more. *Wa-hum yus'alūn* — "but they shall be questioned" — is the verse's pastoral content, and it is not a threat so much as a statement that human action matters. If creatures were never questioned, nothing they did would have consequence, and the moral seriousness the Qur'an insists upon throughout would be unfounded. The clause is therefore the ground of human significance rather than a limit upon it.

Within the sūrah's argument the clause completes the movement begun at verse 21. There the audience was asked whether their gods resurrect; here they are told that they themselves will be asked about their answer. The sūrah does not leave the question rhetorical. It converts the demand for proof at verse 24 into a demand that will be made again, under different circumstances, at a time when evasion is no longer available. The pairing has a rhetorical function as well as a theological one: it answers the objection that the argument is merely intellectual by observing that it will be put to the hearer personally.

What the commentators leave open is how the two halves are to be held together in practice. The verse does not say that the impossibility of questioning God absolves the questioner of responsibility for his own acts; it says the opposite. The asymmetry is not a licence but a specification of where the examination will fall. Verse 24's *Bring me your proof!* is thus not a debating challenge but an advance notice, and verse 23 is the reason it can be issued with confidence rather than as a rhetorical gesture.

---

**The Verse in the Sūrah's Sequence**

Verse 23 sits between two arguments and belongs to both. Verse 22 has established the unity of the source of the world's order; verse 24 will demand proof from those who claim otherwise. The verse between them states the relation in which the demanding and the demanded stand to the One whose unity has just been shown, and without it the demand at verse 24 would lack its ground. One can demand proof of another only where there is a shared accountability, and the verse establishes that there is one.

The commentators' attention to teleology and predestination in this verse is therefore not a digression into systematic theology but a response to the question the sequence raises. If God is not questioned about what He does, in what sense can His creatures be questioned about theirs? The classical answers differ, and the verse does not settle the difference, but the question arises from the verse's position in the argument rather than from the commentators' independent interests.

Read in sequence, the verse also has a rhetorical function. It tells the audience that the demand about to be made at verse 24 is not the last word on the matter. There will be a further questioning, and it will not be conducted in the register of debate. The sūrah thereby converts what could be read as an intellectual dispute into a matter of consequence, and verse 23 is the hinge on which that conversion turns.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:24

> **Or have they taken gods apart from Him? Say, "Bring me your proof! This is the Reminder for those who are with me, and the Reminder for those before me. Nay, but most of them do not know the truth; so they turn away."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Demand for Proof**

The verse returns to the question posed at verse 21 — *Or have they taken gods apart from Him?* — and this time it does not leave the question rhetorical. It converts it into a demand: *Say, "Bring me your proof!"* The Qur'an's use of *burhān* here is technical. The source commentary notes that *burhān* means a demonstration or evidence, and that the word is used in the same sense at 2:111, 23:117, 27:64, and 28:75. The word choice specifies the kind of thing that would satisfy the demand, and the specification is one that ancestral practice cannot meet.

This is a striking move in a text often characterised as demanding submission. The verse does not say *believe because I say so*; it says *produce your demonstration*. It places the burden of argument on the party making the positive claim — that there are gods besides God — and it identifies that burden as one the claimants have never attempted to discharge. The demand is not rhetorical precisely because it has never been met, and its force lies in that fact rather than in any expectation that a demonstration will now be forthcoming.

The instruction *Say* places the demand in the Prophet's mouth rather than in the text's narrative voice, which makes it a public challenge rather than a private reflection. The audience is being addressed directly and is being asked for something specific. This is the sūrah's method at its most explicit: not to assert the absurdity of idolatry but to invite its adherents to state their case, on the confident expectation that no case exists to be stated. The confidence is not arrogance but the result of a demand that has been outstanding since the first messenger.

**Two Reminders**

*This is the Reminder for those who are with me, and the Reminder for those before me.* The word in both instances is *dhikr*, which the commentary notes can mean invoke, mention, recall, remembrance, or even renown. The double occurrence is deliberate: the Prophet ﷺ is told that his own revelation is continuous with what preceded it, not a departure from it, and the continuity is stated twice rather than once so that no reader can take it as incidental. The verse is answering an objection before it is fully voiced.

Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī record two readings. On the first, *Reminder for those before me* refers to the fact that the Qur'an recounts the lives of peoples who passed away, and would be rendered "Reminder *of* those before me." On the second, it refers to the Torah and the Gospel, whose content — like that of the Qur'an — is that human beings should not take gods apart from Him, a point the source commentary notes is reaffirmed at verse 25. Both readings serve the same argumentative purpose, and the commentators record both without adjudicating between them.

That purpose is to establish that the demand for proof is not a novel challenge invented by this messenger but the standing challenge of every messenger, with the earlier scriptures cited as witnesses to it. This answers in advance the objection most natural to an audience that reveres earlier revelations — that the Prophet ﷺ is asking for something no prophet ever asked. By invoking the Torah and the Gospel as containing the same demand, the verse turns the audience's own respect for prior revelation into support for the challenge being made.

**Why They Turn Away**

The verse ends with a diagnosis rather than a conclusion: *Nay, but most of them do not know the truth; so they turn away.* The sequence is important. The turning away is not presented as a considered rejection of evidence but as a consequence of not knowing. The commentators read this as an explanation of the demand for proof in the first clause: one asks for a demonstration from people who have never been asked to provide one, and who have therefore never examined what they hold. The ignorance is not culpable in the first instance; it is inherited and unexamined.

The diagnosis is charitable in a way that is easily overlooked. The verse does not say that they turn away because they are wicked or because they have seen the truth and refused it. It says that they do not know, and that the turning away follows from the not knowing. That is a different claim, and it leaves open the possibility that examination would change the outcome — which is precisely why the demand for proof is issued rather than a verdict pronounced. A polemic against deliberate rejection would look quite different from this.

The verse also establishes something about the structure of religious claims worth stating explicitly. The party asserting the existence of gods besides God bears the burden of proof and has never discharged it; the party denying it does not bear a corresponding burden, because a denial of this kind is not a positive claim requiring evidence. This is why the sūrah can move from the demand at verse 24 to the statement at verse 25 without any gap. Verse 25 does not offer proof of monotheism; it states that every messenger was given the same message. The burden has been placed and remains where it was placed.

---

**The Demand and the Audience's Own Scriptures**

The verse's invocation of the Reminder for those before me has a specific polemical use that is easy to miss. The Meccan audience included people who knew the Torah and the Gospel by reputation and who treated both as revelations. By citing them as containing the same demand, the verse places the audience in the position of having to choose between their reverence for those scriptures and their practice at home. The choice is not forced rhetorically; it is presented as a matter of fact.

Al-Rāzī's reading — that the content of the earlier Reminder, like that of the Qur'an, is that human beings should not take gods apart from Him — makes the point sharply. If the earlier scriptures say what the Qur'an says on this matter, then the audience's appeal to those scriptures is an appeal against their own practice rather than in defence of it. The verse does not need to argue for the authority of the Torah and the Gospel; it needs only to note what they contain.

This is why the verse can issue its demand so plainly. It is not asking the audience to accept a new authority but to apply one they already accept. The *burhān* demanded is not a demonstration internal to Islam but the demonstration that any of the revelations would require, and the verse's confidence rests on the fact that the demand has been made before and has never been met by anyone claiming gods besides God.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:25

> **And We sent no messenger before thee, save that We revealed unto him, "Verily, there is no god but I; so worship Me!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Message, Many Messengers**

*And We sent no messenger before thee, save that We revealed unto him, "Verily, there is no god but I; so worship Me!"* Al-Ṭabarī's note identifies the verse's precise claim: even though the messengers had different commands regarding what was obligatory and forbidden, they all conveyed the same message of monotheism and devotion to God. The distinction is essential to the verse's logic and is frequently lost in summary. The sūrah is not saying that all religions have the same law; it is saying that all revelation has the same first principle, and that the law varied while the principle did not.

Al-Qurṭubī adds that this confirms the self-evident existence and Oneness of God. The phrase *self-evident* does real work here. If tawḥīd required demonstration in each generation, it would be a discovery rather than a reminder; the verse's insistence that every messenger received it as revelation implies that it is being restored rather than invented, and that the need for restoration is a fact about human forgetfulness rather than about the obscurity of the truth. The verse's grammar enacts the point by giving the revealed words in the first person.

Because the words are *there is no god but I; so worship Me*, every messenger speaks the same sentence in the same voice. The variation lies entirely in the law that accompanies it, and the verse is explicit that the variation was real — the messengers had different commands regarding what was obligatory and forbidden. What did not vary is the object of worship or the requirement that He be worshipped. The commentators direct the reader to the essay "The Quranic View of Sacred History and Other Religions" on these fundamental similarities among the messages and messengers.

**The Grammatical Variant and Its Effect**

The source commentary records that some read part of the verse in the third person: *We sent no messenger before thee, but that it was revealed unto him*. The variant shifts the address from the Prophet ﷺ to a general statement about prophethood, and in doing so removes any suggestion that the second-person address singles him out. On either reading the content is identical; what changes is whether the verse is heard as reassurance to one man or as a description of the office he holds. Both readings are recorded without preference.

Both support the argument the sūrah has been making since verse 21. The demand for proof at verse 24 was not a personal challenge but the standing challenge of every messenger, and verse 25 establishes that standing by tracing it to the origin of the office itself. The third-person reading makes this more visible by removing the personal address altogether and leaving only the structure of the office. The second-person reading has a pastoral use: it comforts a man being told that he has brought something unprecedented.

The variant therefore does not create a difficulty but supplies two registers of the same claim. Read in the second person, the verse reassures; read in the third, it demonstrates. The sūrah needs both, because the audience's objection has both a personal and a general form — that this particular man is out of step with his ancestors, and that his message contradicts what came before. The first objection is answered by the reassurance, the second by the genealogy.

**Worship as the Consequence of Tawḥīd**

The revealed sentence has two halves joined by *fa-* — *there is no god but I; so worship Me* — and the conjunction indicates consequence rather than mere sequence. Worship follows from the recognition of unity; it is not an additional requirement laid on top of it. This is the same structure al-Sulamī identifies at verse 22, where the unity of God and the unity of the heart are the same truth at two scales. The verse therefore does not present monotheism as a proposition to be assented to and worship as a separate obligation, but as one movement issuing in the other.

The messengers differed in what that worship required in detail; they did not differ in the fact that it followed from the recognition. Read against the preceding verses, this explains why the sūrah treats idolatry as an intellectual error rather than merely a ritual one. The failure at verse 21 and verse 24 is a failure to recognise unity, and the worship of stones is its consequence. Verse 25 states the correct order so that the incorrect one can be seen as a reversal of it rather than as an independent choice.

The verse closes one argument and opens another. It answers the claim that the Prophet ﷺ has brought something unprecedented by showing that his message is the oldest there is, and it then sets up verse 26, which records a specific departure from that message: *And they say, "The Compassionate has taken a child."* The juxtaposition is the point. Verse 25 states what every messenger was given; verse 26 states what some have said against it. The verse is not a claim that all religious traditions as currently practised are equivalent, but a claim about what was originally revealed to each — which leaves open the question of what has been preserved, the question the sūrah pursues next.

---

**Why the Verse Is Placed Here**

The verse's position is argumentative rather than incidental. Four verses of demand and diagnosis precede it, and the natural objection to all four is that the Prophet ﷺ is asking his audience to abandon the religion of their fathers for something he has invented. Verse 25 removes that objection at its root by showing that what he brings is not new but is what every messenger was given, and that the religion of the fathers is the departure rather than the inheritance.

The reversal is the verse's central rhetorical move. An audience that thinks of itself as conservative is told that it is the innovator, and the evidence offered is the record of the messengers rather than any claim about the present. This is why the verse gives the revealed words in the first person and traces them to every messenger without exception: the argument depends on the universality of the formula and would be weakened by any acknowledged variation in it.

The verse also prepares the transition to verse 26, which records a specific departure from the formula. Having established what was revealed to every messenger, the sūrah can present the claim that the Compassionate has taken a child not as an alternative tradition but as a contradiction of the tradition both parties accept. The juxtaposition does the work that an argument would otherwise have to do, and it is the reason verse 25 is placed immediately before it.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:26

> **And they say, "The Compassionate has taken a child." Glory be to Him! Nay, but they are honored servants.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Claims Behind One Sentence**

*And they say, "The Compassionate has taken a child." Glory be to Him!* The source commentary identifies two distinct beliefs behind the single accusation. Among the idolatrous Quraysh, the claim was that the angels were daughters of God, as at 37:149–53 and 43:16–17. Among Christians, it was the doctrine of the Divine sonship of Jesus, as at 4:171. The verse addresses both without naming either, because the objection it raises applies equally to both and does not depend on which is intended.

Al-Qurṭubī records a specific occasion of revelation: the verse came in connection with the tribe of Khuzāʿah, who considered the angels to be daughters of God and worshipped them in order to obtain their intercession. That detail explains the shape of the rebuttal. The verse does not argue about genealogy or about the possibility of Divine offspring in the abstract; it identifies the angels as *honored servants*, which answers the practice rather than the theory.

The use of *al-Raḥmān* — the Compassionate — in reporting the claim is worth noticing. It is the name the sūrah has used of God throughout, and placing it in the mouth of the claimants makes the accusation concrete rather than generic. They are not speaking about a distant deity but about the One the sūrah has been describing, and the *Glory be to Him* that follows is therefore a rejection of something said about a specific and named subject.

**Why Begetting Is Impossible**

The commentary records a nuance that is easily missed. Some Muslim theologians do not object to calling God "Father" in a narrow metaphorical or symbolic sense; what they reject is the assertion that a Divinely begotten being would differ ontologically from other creatures. The objection is not to the word but to the claim of a different order of being, and the distinction is preserved carefully in the classical discussion.

The theological ground is the one established at verse 22. If God is one and uncomposed, then begetting would introduce distinction within the Divine and would make the begotten either a creature or a second god. Neither is available. The verse's *Glory be to Him* — *subḥānahu* — is the standard Qur'anic marker that a statement has attributed to God something incompatible with His unity, and it appears here for exactly that reason.

It follows that the verse is not making an empirical claim about angels or about Jesus but a structural one about what divinity cannot include. This is why the rebuttal can be so short. Once the unity argued at verse 22 is granted, the claim that God has taken a child is not merely false but incoherent, and the sūrah treats it as such rather than laboring the point.

**The Sectarian Error and the Evidence of Behaviour**

The commentary draws a parallel that broadens the verse's scope. A similar error is to believe that people are children of God in an exclusive and sectarian way, as at 5:18: *And the Jews and the Christians say, "We are the children of God, and His beloved ones." Say, "Why then does He punish you for your sins?" Nay, but you are mortals of His creating.* The rebuttal there is not metaphysical but practical: a claim to exclusive Divine favour is tested by whether its holders are exempt from accountability.

Verse 27 then supplies the evidence that settles the matter about the angels: *They precede Him not in speech, and they act according to His Command.* A child acts on its own initiative; a servant acts on command. The sūrah settles the question not by argument about nature but by observing behaviour, and the behaviour is described in the following verse rather than asserted in this one.

The two verses together form a single argument. Verse 26 states the claim and rejects it; verse 27 gives the reason the rejection is warranted. The Khuzāʿah's practice was incoherent because it presupposed that the angels could act on their own behalf and on behalf of others, and verse 27 states that they cannot. The verse's brevity is therefore not economy but confidence in the evidence to come.

---

**What the Verse Leaves Undecided**

The verse rejects the claim that the Compassionate has taken a child without specifying which of the two beliefs it has in view, and the commentators are content to leave it that way. This is not evasion. The objection — that begetting would introduce distinction into a God who is one and uncomposed — applies to the Quraysh's daughters and to the Christian sonship alike, and naming either would narrow an argument that does not need narrowing.

There is a further reason for the generality. The verse is addressed to an audience that includes both claimants, and a rebuttal aimed at one would leave the other free to suppose itself exempt. By stating the objection at the level of structure rather than of sect, the verse closes both routes at once, and the *Glory be to Him* covers whichever claim the hearer happens to hold.

The generality also has a consequence for how the verse has been read historically. Because it does not name its target, it has been applied to any claim of Divine offspring, and the commentators' treatment of 5:18 — where the claim is made by people rather than about a person — shows that they understood the error to have more than one form. The verse identifies a category, not an incident.

---

**The Name and the Claim**

One further detail deserves attention. The claim is reported with the Divine name *al-Raḥmān*, and the sūrah uses that name deliberately. It is the name under which God has been described in the preceding verses — the One to whom belongs whosoever is in the heavens and the earth, whose servants do not weary in His glorification. The accusation is therefore not against an abstract deity but against the specific subject the sūrah has been describing, and the *Glory be to Him* is a rejection of something said about Him by name.

The commentators note that the Qur'an returns to this accusation at several points, always with the same formula of rejection. The recurrence indicates that the claim was live rather than hypothetical in the audience the sūrah addresses, and it explains why the rebuttal here is structural rather than evidential. There is no observation that could settle whether God has offspring; what settles it is the unity already established, and the sūrah relies on that rather than attempting anything else.

This is also why the verse moves so quickly to the angels' status. Having rejected the claim, it does not linger on the rejection but supplies the positive account: *Nay, but they are honored servants*. The correction is as important as the denial, because the Khuzāʿah's error was not merely that they asserted something false about God but that they misdescribed the beings they were worshipping, and the misdescription is what verse 27 proceeds to correct in detail.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:27

> **They precede Him not in speech, and they act according to His Command.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Speech Under Command**

*Lā yasbiqūnahu bi'l-qawli wa-hum bi-amrihi yaʿmalūn.* "They precede Him not in speech, and they act according to His Command." Al-Ṭabarī explains the first clause directly: the angels do not speak except as commanded by God and do not act except according to His Will. The verb *yasbiqūna* — "they precede" — is chosen with precision, and the precision is where the verse's argument lives.

The verse does not say that the angels do not speak; it says they do not speak *first*. That is a claim about temporal and causal order rather than about silence. The angels are not mute; they are responsive. Every utterance they make is a response to a command that has already been given, and the verse's concern is to establish that there is no initiative in them that originates from themselves.

This is what makes the clause probative for the argument begun at verse 26. A being who never speaks before God speaks is a being with no independent will, and therefore no claim to divinity. The evidence offered is not metaphysical but observable in the description of how these beings operate, which is the method the sūrah has used since verse 21 — appeal to what can be seen rather than to what must be assumed.

**The Two Halves of the Verse**

The verse pairs speech and action, and the pairing covers the whole of agency. What a being says and what it does are the two modes in which initiative appears, and by denying the angels priority in the first and subordinating them in the second, the verse leaves no residue of independent will. This is what makes the rebuttal of verse 26 complete rather than merely assertive.

It also explains why the Khuzāʿah's practice was incoherent. They worshipped the angels to obtain intercession, which presupposes that the angels can act on their own initiative and on behalf of others. Verse 27 states that they cannot: they act according to His Command. Verse 28 then makes the point explicit with regard to intercession itself, so that the three verses form a single continuous argument rather than three separate assertions.

The structure of the verse mirrors the structure of the claim it answers. The claim was that the angels are children of God; the answer is that they are servants, and the evidence for servitude is the absence of precedence in both speech and action. The commentators do not treat the two clauses as independent observations but as two halves of one description, and the description is the proof.

**Al-Sulamī's Inward Modelling**

The commentary records al-Sulamī's spiritual reading: one should inwardly model oneself on the angels, striving neither to speak nor to act except in accordance with the Will of God, adhering to the Sunnah, and being with God constantly. The reading does not soften the verse's theological claim; it makes it available as a discipline, and the discipline is exacting rather than comforting.

The move is characteristic of the Sufi exegesis the commentators record elsewhere in this sūrah. A statement about angelic ontology becomes a rule for human conduct, and the rule is not that one should speak and act *mostly* in accordance with God's Will but that one should not precede Him in either. What is impossible for human beings to achieve perfectly is set as the direction of effort rather than as a measurable standard.

There is a connection to verse 20 worth noting. The angels' glorification was described there as their breathing — not an act performed between other acts but a mode of existence. Al-Sulamī's gloss asks the reader to move in that direction, not by adding acts but by changing the relation in which his acts stand. The verse thereby supplies both the theological answer to verse 26 and a practical aim for the reader.

---

**Why the Verse Matters to the Argument**

Verse 27 is short and easily treated as a descriptive aside, but it carries the evidential weight of the whole angelic sequence. Verses 26 and 28 make claims about what the angels are and what they may do; verse 27 supplies the observation on which both claims rest. Without it, the sequence would be a set of assertions about celestial beings whose behaviour nobody can inspect.

The verse's method is to describe operation rather than nature. It does not say that the angels are servants; it says that they do not speak first and that they act on command. The inference to servitude is left to the reader, which is the sūrah's consistent practice — state what can be observed, and let the conclusion follow rather than announcing it.

This is also why al-Sulamī's reading is possible. A description of operation can be taken as a rule for conduct in a way that a statement of nature cannot. If the verse had said only that the angels are servants, there would be nothing for a reader to imitate; because it describes how they speak and act, it supplies a pattern, and the pattern is what the spiritual reading develops.

---

**The Verse and the Question of Intercession**

Verse 27 does the preparatory work that makes verse 28's treatment of intercession possible. The Khuzāʿah worshipped the angels in order to obtain their intercession, which presupposes that the angels have discretion in the matter — that they can be cultivated, appealed to, and prevailed upon. Verse 27 removes the presupposition by stating that the angels do not act except according to His Command, and verse 28 then states the consequence for intercession specifically.

The order is significant. If verse 28 had come first, the limit on intercession would have appeared as an arbitrary restriction. Coming after verse 27, it appears as a consequence of the angels' general subordination, which is what the argument requires. The commentators treat the two verses as a unit for this reason, and al-Ṭabarī's gloss on verse 27 — that the angels neither speak nor act except as commanded — is the premise from which the rest follows.

There is a pastoral dimension to the sequence as well. A believer who understands that the angels act only on command cannot treat them as independent patrons, and the understanding closes off a practice that the sūrah regards as a corruption of honour into worship. The verse is therefore not only a theological statement but a correction of practice, and its brevity reflects the fact that the correction follows immediately from the description.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:28

> **He knows that which is before them and that which is behind them, and they intercede not, save for one with whom He is content. They are wary, for fear of Him.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Knowledge Before and Behind**

*Yaʿlamu mā bayna aydīhim wa-mā khalfahum.* "He knows that which is before them and that which is behind them." Al-Ṭabarī reads the phrase as what will take place in the future and what took place in the past; al-Qurṭubī offers the Hereafter and this world. Both readings make the same point about the angels' position: they are situated within time and within knowledge, not outside either.

The clause is placed first in the verse for a reason. Before the verse addresses intercession, it establishes that God's knowledge encompasses the angels entirely. What follows about intercession is therefore not a restriction imposed from without but a consequence of a knowledge that already contains them. The ordering is argumentative rather than merely descriptive.

This is the same move the sūrah made at verse 23. There it was established that God is not questioned about what He does while creatures are questioned; here it is established that the angels, though the highest of creatures, fall wholly within His knowledge. The two verses together fix the angels' position in the order of things, and the fixing is what makes the rest of the verse follow.

**The Conditions on Intercession**

*Wa-lā yashfaʿūna illā li-mani'rtaḍā.* "They intercede not, save for one with whom He is content." The source commentary directs the reader to the Qur'anic treatment of intercession at 2:48 and 2:255, noting that it is usually framed in terms of whom God allows to intercede on the Day of Judgment. The verse therefore does not deny intercession; it denies that intercession is available at the intercessor's discretion.

Al-Rāzī connects the clause to a live theological dispute. For some commentators, *one with whom He is content* includes anyone who believes *There is no god but God*; for the Muʿtazilites, it is restricted to those who believe and have not committed major sins. The verse does not decide the question, and the commentators record both positions rather than harmonising them, which is itself informative about how the classical tradition read it.

What both positions accept is that the scope of intercession is fixed by God's contentment and not by the intercessor's favour. That common ground is what the verse needs for its argument, since the Khuzāʿah's practice assumed precisely the opposite — that the angels could be cultivated for their intercession. The verse removes the assumption without needing to settle the wider question about major sin.

**Wariness as the Mark of a Creature**

The verse closes: *They are wary, for fear of Him.* Al-Rāzī draws the inference that settles the argument begun at verse 26. The attributes of fear and wariness — the latter also renderable as anxiety — are not those of a Divinely begotten being but of a creature who knows that God is omniscient and omnipotent. The commentary notes that *wariness* carries the sense of being anxious and careful as well as cautious.

This is the sūrah's method at its most economical. Rather than argue from the nature of the Divine, it observes the disposition of the angels and reads their nature off it. A son does not stand in anxious fear of his father's knowledge. The beings whom the Khuzāʿah worshipped as daughters of God are described as standing in exactly that posture, and the description is the refutation.

The three verses therefore close as a unit. Verse 26 reports the claim and rejects it; verse 27 describes the angels' obedience in speech and action; verse 28 describes their knowledge-bounded position, the limits on their intercession, and their wariness. What emerges is not a denunciation of the angels but an account of them so precise that the claim made on their behalf cannot survive it. The sūrah honours them by describing them accurately.

---

**The Verse as a Whole**

Read as a unit, verse 28 does three things in sequence: it locates the angels within God's knowledge, it limits their intercession to those with whom He is content, and it describes their wariness. The sequence is not a list of attributes but an argument, and each clause prepares the next. Knowledge establishes the relation; the limit on intercession follows from it; the wariness is the disposition that results.

The commentators' attention to the Muʿtazilite dispute over major sin shows that they read the middle clause as doing real theological work rather than as a formula. The dispute is about the scope of *one with whom He is content*, and the fact that it was disputed indicates that the clause was understood as substantive. What was not disputed — that the scope is God's to fix — is what the verse requires.

The closing clause then supplies the affective dimension that the preceding two lack. The angels are not merely bounded and limited; they are aware of being so, and the awareness takes the form of wariness. Al-Rāzī's inference rests on this: fear and anxiety are the marks of a creature, and their presence in the description settles the question verse 26 raised without any further argument being needed.

---

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

The verse limits intercession but does not deny it, and the distinction has mattered considerably in the subsequent tradition. *They intercede not, save for one with whom He is content* presupposes that intercession occurs; what it denies is that the intercessor chooses its objects. The commentators' cross-references to 2:48 and 2:255 confirm that this is the standard Qur'anic framing, and the verse is consistent with it rather than exceptional to it.

Nor does the verse say that the angels are unaware or uncomprehending. *He knows that which is before them and that which is behind them* establishes that God's knowledge exceeds theirs, not that they have none. The wariness described at the end is the disposition of beings who know a great deal and know that it is bounded, which is a different thing from ignorance and is what makes the wariness meaningful.

These negatives matter because the verse is easily read as a denigration of the angels, and it is not. It honours them by describing them precisely: knowledgeable but bounded, powerful in intercession but not discretionary, honoured but not divine. The precision is what defeats the claim made on their behalf, and a vague denunciation would not have served the argument at all.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:29

> **And whosoever among them would say, "Truly I am a god apart from Him," such will We requite with Hell. Thus do We requite the wrongdoers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Conditional, Not a Report**

*And whosoever among them would say, "Truly I am a god apart from Him," such will We requite with Hell.* The verse is a conditional. The source commentary records a disagreement about how to read it: some commentators take it as a specific reference to Iblīs, while others say that it does not describe something anyone actually said but states what would happen if they did.

Al-Rāzī's reading is the more consequential. On his account the verse is not a historical report but a rule, and its force lies in the fact that no angel has ever made the claim. The verse establishes the penalty in order to make the absence of the crime meaningful: these beings are under the same sanction as every other creature, and their obedience is obedience under sanction rather than obedience by nature.

The reference to Iblīs serves the same purpose from the opposite side. Iblīs appears in the Qur'anic account of the prostration among the angels, and he did make a claim of superiority. The verse's conditional therefore has an instance, and the instance shows that the penalty is not hypothetical. Both readings are preserved by the commentators because both are needed — the rule and its one exception.

**Why the Penalty Must Be Stated**

The argument of verses 26–28 depends on this verse to be complete. If the angels were incapable of the claim by nature, their obedience would carry no merit and their servitude would be no evidence against their divinity. By stating the penalty, the verse establishes that the claim was available to them and was not made — which is precisely what makes their behaviour probative rather than merely automatic.

This is a subtle point and the commentators handle it carefully. The wariness described at verse 28 is the disposition of a being who knows the sanction; the conditional at verse 29 states the sanction. Without the second, the first would be unexplained, and without the first, the second would be a bare threat. The two verses support each other, and the argument they carry is that angelic obedience is chosen rather than compelled.

It also completes the rebuttal of verse 26. A being who could claim divinity and does not is a servant; a being who could not claim it is a mechanism. The sūrah's description of the angels requires the first, and verse 29 supplies it. The Khuzāʿah's error was therefore not that they honoured the angels too highly but that they mistook the character of that honour.

**"Thus Do We Requite the Wrongdoers"**

The closing clause generalises: *Thus do We requite the wrongdoers.* The shift from the specific case to the class is significant. The penalty for claiming divinity is not a special sanction reserved for celestial beings; it is the standard requital for wrongdoing, and the claim to be a god apart from God is presented as wrongdoing in its most complete form.

This connects the verse to the sūrah's larger pattern. From verse 21 onward the argument has proceeded by showing that the gods taken from the earth cannot perform the functions of a god. Verse 29 completes it by showing that even the highest of creatures would be requited for claiming the office. The conclusion the sūrah leaves is not that the angels are unworthy of honour but that honour and divinity are different things, and that confusing them is the error the sūrah has been tracing.

The generalisation also extends the verse beyond the angels. If the penalty is the requital of wrongdoers, then it applies to any claimant, celestial or terrestrial, and the idols of verse 21 fall under it as certainly as an angel would. The verse thus closes the angelic sequence and returns the argument to its original subject, having established that the standard applies universally.

---

**The Verse's Place in the Sequence**

Verses 26 to 29 form a complete argument, and verse 29 is its closing move. Verse 26 reports the claim that the Compassionate has taken a child and rejects it. Verse 27 describes the angels' subordination in speech and action. Verse 28 describes their bounded knowledge, the limits on their intercession, and their wariness. Verse 29 states what would happen if any of them claimed divinity, and thereby shows that the claim was possible and was not made.

That last point is what converts the sequence from description into evidence. A being described as subordinate might be subordinate by nature, in which case the description would prove nothing about divinity. A being described as subordinate *under sanction* is choosing, and the choice is what makes the behaviour probative. The commentators' disagreement about whether the verse refers to Iblīs is a disagreement about how the point is best made, not about whether it is made.

The sequence also closes by returning to the sūrah's original subject. The generalising clause — *Thus do We requite the wrongdoers* — extends the penalty beyond the angels to any claimant, which brings the idols of verse 21 back into view. What began as a question about gods taken from the earth ends with a statement that applies to every claim of divinity, celestial or terrestrial, without exception.

---

**Reading the Conditional Against the Sequence**

The conditional form of the verse has a function that becomes clear only when it is read against verses 26 to 28. Those verses describe the angels as they are: subordinate in speech and action, bounded in knowledge, limited in intercession, wary before God. A reader might conclude that they are simply constituted this way, in which case their behaviour would carry no evidence about the claim made on their behalf.

Verse 29 forecloses that conclusion by stating the penalty for a claim they have not made. The penalty is meaningful only if the claim was available, and its availability means the behaviour described in the preceding verses is chosen rather than compelled. The commentators' disagreement about whether the verse refers to Iblīs concerns the instance, not the logic, and both readings preserve the point.

This is why the verse is placed last in the sequence rather than first. Read first, it would be a bare threat with no context. Read last, it supplies the missing premise that converts three verses of description into evidence, and its generalising close — *Thus do We requite the wrongdoers* — extends the conclusion to every claimant, which returns the argument to the idols with which it began at verse 21.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:30

> **Have those who disbelieve not considered that the heavens and the earth were sewn together and We rent them asunder? And We made every living thing from water. Will they not, then, believe?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rending Asunder**

*Have those who disbelieve not considered that the heavens and the earth were sewn together and We rent them asunder?* The verse's opening — *a-wa-lam yara* — is an appeal to observation, not to revelation. The sūrah asks its audience to consider something they can see, and then draws a conclusion they have been resisting. The method is the one used at verse 21, where the audience was asked whether gods taken from the earth can resurrect.

The source commentary records four readings of the rending. Some see it as God's making the earth into seven earths and the heaven into seven heavens, alluded to at 65:12. Others read it as the pulling apart of the heavens and the earth, which were originally connected or adjacent. Al-Qurṭubī offers that they were a single thing which God separated with air and wind. A fourth reading, preferred by some because of the phrase that follows, holds that the heavens are rent asunder by rain and the earth by the vegetation that grows from it.

The commentators record all four without ranking them decisively, and the plurality is instructive. The verse's terms — *ratq* and *fatq* — are general enough to bear physical, cosmological, and ongoing readings at once. What the readings share is that the separation is an act rather than a given, and that it is attributable to God, which is what the verse needs for the question it closes with.

**Abū Muslim al-Iṣfahānī's Reading**

Al-Rāzī preserves an interpretation that differs in kind from the others. For Abū Muslim al-Iṣfahānī, *sewn together* (*ratq*) symbolises the state of things before creation, where nothingness was all of a piece and nothing could be distinguished from anything else; *rent asunder* (*fatq*) symbolises the act of creation itself, in which realities once joined in nonexistence come to be separate as they take on existence and individual identity.

On this reading the verse is not describing a physical event but the structure of creation as such. Individuation *is* the rending. To exist is to be distinguished, and the distinction is not something added to existence but is what existence consists in. The commentators record this alongside the physical readings without ranking it against them, treating it as a different level of the same claim rather than a rival account.

The reading has a consequence for how the verse's question is to be taken. If the rending is creation as such, then the audience is not being asked to consider a historical event they cannot witness but the condition of anything existing at all — including themselves. The appeal to observation is then not to the sky but to the fact of distinction, which is available to anyone at any moment.

**Water and the Closing Question**

*And We made every living thing from water.* The commentators note that this phrase is one reason the rain-and-vegetation reading of the rending is preferred: the verse moves from separation to life, and water is the link. The claim is universal — *every living thing* — and it is stated without qualification in a desert society for which water was the obvious limit on life.

The verse ends: *Will they not, then, believe?* The *then* is consequential. The sūrah has not offered a proof from authority but an observation from experience, and the question asks why the observation does not produce the conclusion. This is the same method as verse 21, and the recurrence establishes the sūrah's approach: the evidence is already in hand and has simply not been applied.

Read against verse 25, the verse also answers the objection that the Prophet ﷺ brings something unprecedented. What he brings is a reminder, and the reminder is of things the audience can already see — the separation of the heavens and the earth, the dependence of every living thing on water. The demand at verse 24 for proof is met here not with an argument but with an invitation to look.

---

**The Verse's Argumentative Role**

Verse 30 marks a shift in the sūrah's method. From verse 21 to verse 29 the argument has concerned worship and the beings worshipped; from verse 30 it concerns the physical world. The shift is not a change of subject but a change of evidence, and it comes at the point where the preceding arguments have exhausted what can be said about the angels and the idols.

The new evidence has a distinctive advantage: it requires no premises the audience disputes. Nobody in Mecca doubted that the heavens and the earth are distinct, or that living things depend on water. The verse therefore does not need to establish its observations, only to ask what follows from them, and the question at the end — *Will they not, then, believe?* — assumes that the audience already has what it needs.

This is the same structure as verse 24's demand for proof. There the audience was asked to produce a demonstration for a claim it had never supported; here it is asked to draw a conclusion from an observation it has never disputed. In both cases the sūrah's confidence rests not on the strength of its own argument but on the fact that the audience's position has never been examined.

---

**The Question and Its Audience**

The closing question — *Will they not, then, believe?* — is addressed to *those who disbelieve*, named in the verse's opening words. The audience is therefore not neutral; they are people who have already declined to believe, and the verse asks why the evidence in front of them has not changed that. The question is not rhetorical in the sense of expecting no answer; it expects an answer the audience cannot give.

The commentators' note that the rain-and-vegetation reading is preferred because of the water clause indicates that they read the verse as a connected whole rather than as two separate claims. The separation of the heavens and the earth, the making of every living thing from water, and the closing question form one movement: here is what you can see, here is what it depends on, and here is the conclusion you have not drawn.

The verse's confidence rests on the availability of its evidence. Unlike a claim about the angels or about the afterlife, the dependence of living things on water can be checked by anyone in a desert society, at any time, without instruction. The sūrah chooses this evidence deliberately, at the point where the preceding arguments have run out of observations the audience shares, and it is the first of several such appeals in the verses that follow.

---
## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:31

> **And We placed firm mountains in the earth, lest it shake beneath them, and We made wide tracts between them as paths, that haply they may be guided.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Mountains as Pegs**

*And We placed firm mountains in the earth, lest it shake beneath them.* The verse presents the mountains as functional rather than decorative: they were placed, they have a purpose, and the purpose is the stability of the ground beneath human beings. The Qur'an returns to this image repeatedly, and its force in a seismically active region is immediate rather than theoretical.

The commentators read the verse within the same framework as verse 30. Where verse 30 described the separation of the heavens and the earth, verse 31 describes the settling of the earth after that separation. The sequence is creation as a series of deliberate acts, each addressing a condition that would otherwise make the world uninhabitable, and the pairing of the two verses is deliberate.

The verb *jaʿalnā* — "We placed" or "We made" — is the same verb used of the water in the preceding verse. The connection is not accidental. Both verses describe provisions made for living things, and both are offered as evidence rather than as information. The audience is not being told something new about mountains but being asked to see what they already know about them as provision.

**Wide Tracts as Paths**

*And We made wide tracts between them as paths, that haply they may be guided.* The second half of the verse is often passed over, and it is the more interesting. The mountains are not merely stabilisers; the spaces between them are corridors. The verse describes a landscape that is both fixed and traversable — anchored enough to stand on, open enough to travel through.

The word for wide tracts, *fijāj*, denotes routes between mountains. The verse's concern is practical: the Meccan audience were traders whose livelihood depended on passes through the Hijaz. The sūrah points to the infrastructure of their own commerce as evidence of deliberate design, which is the same rhetorical method as verse 30's appeal to water.

There is a structural elegance in the pairing that is worth noticing. The mountains make the earth stable; the passes make it navigable. Stability without passes would be a prison, and passes without stability would be chaos. The verse presents both as provisions, and the combination is what makes the earth usable rather than merely existent.

**"That Haply They May Be Guided"**

The clause *laʿallahum yahtadūn* — "that haply they may be guided" — carries a double sense that the verse leaves open. Guidance here can mean wayfinding, the physical navigation of a route; it can also mean the guidance that is the sūrah's central concern. The commentators do not resolve the ambiguity, and the verse gains from both readings being available at once.

The pairing is characteristic of the sūrah's argument from verses 30 onward. Having established that God's sovereignty is visible in the structure of the world, it treats the world as navigable in both senses: a traveller who reads the passes correctly arrives, and a reader who reads the same landscape correctly believes. The physical guidance is the visible case of the spiritual one, and the verse's closing clause holds them together without collapsing either into the other.

The word *laʿalla* — "that haply" — is also worth attending to. It indicates a purpose that is offered rather than guaranteed, which preserves the freedom the sūrah has insisted on throughout. The passes are made; whether the traveller uses them is another matter. This is the same structure as verse 24's demand for proof, where the evidence is placed before the audience and the response is left to them.

---

**Provision as Evidence**

Verse 31 completes the argument begun at verse 30 by moving from the fact of creation to its character. The heavens and the earth were separated; the earth was stabilised; routes were made through it; and all of it was done *that haply they may be guided*. The sequence presents creation not as a neutral fact but as an arrangement with the inhabitants in view.

The commentators' preference for the rain-and-vegetation reading of verse 30 supports this. If the rending is ongoing rather than a single primordial event, then provision is continuous, and the mountains and passes of verse 31 are part of the same continuous arrangement rather than a separate act. The two verses together describe a world that is being maintained, not merely made.

The closing clause leaves the response open, and the openness is the point. Provision does not compel belief; it makes belief available. This is consistent with the sūrah's treatment throughout — the demand for proof at verse 24, the question at verse 30, the *haply* at verse 31 — and it explains why the sūrah argues at all rather than simply asserting. What is being offered is evidence, and evidence can be declined.

---

**The Verse in the Sūrah's Argument**

Verse 31 is the second of the sūrah's appeals to the physical world, and it extends the first. Verse 30 asked the audience to consider the separation of the heavens and the earth and the dependence of life on water; verse 31 asks them to consider the stability of the ground beneath them and the routes through it. Both appeals are to things the audience experiences daily, and neither requires premises they would dispute.

The pairing is also a pairing of functions. Verse 30 concerns the conditions of life; verse 31 concerns the conditions of habitation and movement. Together they describe a world arranged for its inhabitants rather than merely containing them, and the closing clause of each — *Will they not, then, believe?* and *that haply they may be guided* — leaves the response with the audience rather than compelling it.

This is the sūrah's method from verse 30 onward, and it explains the shift from the preceding section. The arguments about the angels and the idols depended on descriptions the audience might contest; the arguments about mountains, passes, water, and the alternation of night and day do not. The sūrah moves to the evidence that cannot be declined, and verse 31 is part of that movement.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:32

> **And We made the sky a canopy preserved; yet they turn away from its signs.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"A Canopy Preserved" — The Sky as the Protection**

The thirty-second verse is the sūrah's statement of the sign of the sky, and it is one of the most important verses in the sūrah's account of the signs in creation. *Wa-jaʿalnā al-samaʾa saqfan muḥfaẓan* — "And We made the sky a canopy preserved" — the sūrah is describing the sky not as a mere feature of the cosmos but as the protection, the canopy, the thing that covers and preserves. The root of *saqf* is s-q-f, the root of the roof, of the ceiling, of the canopy. The sūrah is using the word to state that the sky is the canopy, and the canopy is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the protection is the standing order.

The word *muḥfaẓan* — "preserved" — is the word of the protection, the word of the preservation. The source records the commentators' readings: "preserved" means preserved from the satans (the reading of al-Rāzī and several others), or it connotes "upraised," meaning protected from falling down to the earth (the reading of the others). Both readings are sound, and both point to the same theological point: the sky is the protection, and the protection is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the protection is the standing order.

The parallel the source notes is the family of verses that describe the sky as the canopy: 2:22, 40:64, 79:28. The family of verses is constant: the canopy is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the protection is the standing order. The sūrah's use of the word *saqf* is therefore not a description of a one-time event; it is a description of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the protection is the standing order.

**"Preserved" — The Two Readings and Their Theological Weight**

The word *muḥfaẓan* — "preserved" — is the word that carries the theological weight of the verse, and the two readings of the word point to the same theological point. The first reading, recorded in the source, is that the sky is preserved from the satans — that is, that the sky is the protection from the satans, and the protection from the satans is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the protection is the standing order.

The hadith that states the model in its most explicit form is:

> *"The stars are lights that adorn the heavens of the world, and they are also stones with which the devils are pelted."* (al-Bukhārī, in the Book of Tafsīr)

The hadith's logic is the sūrah's logic: the sky is the protection from the satans, and the protection from the satans is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the protection is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the sky is a mere feature; it is that the sky is the protection, and the protection is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the protection is the standing order.

The second reading, recorded in the source, is that the sky is preserved from falling down to the earth — that is, that the sky is the protection from the falling, and the protection from the falling is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the protection is the standing order. The modern mind finds in this reading a resonance with what it now discovers: the atmosphere is the protection from the meteorites, from the radiation, from the things that would destroy the life on the earth. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the sky is a mere feature; it is that the sky is the protection, and the protection is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the protection is the standing order.

**"Yet They Turn Away from Its Signs" — The Refusal of the Sign**

The second half of the verse — *wa-hum ʿāyātihī muʿriḍūn* — "yet they turn away from its signs" — is the sūrah's statement of the refusal of the sign. The sūrah is not describing a failure of the evidence; the sūrah is describing a failure of the consideration, and the consideration is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the refusal is the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that the idolaters turn away in the face of the signs of the heavens, including the sun, moon, and stars. The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the signs are the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the refusal is the standing order. The sūrah's use of the phrase *wa-hum ʿāyātihī muʿriūn* is therefore not a description of a one-time event; it is a description of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the refusal is the standing order.

The parallel the source notes is the sūrah's own use of the word *muʿriḍūn* — "turning away" — which is the same word the sūrah used in verse 1 for the heedless. The sūrah's use of the word in this verse is the return of the word, and the return is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the refusal is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the refusal is the standing order. The reader is meant to hear the verse as a description of the refusal, and the refusal is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the refusal is the standing order.

**The Modern Echo: The Canopy and the Ignorance**

The verse's teaching for the modern reader is the one the sūrah's context makes plain. The modern person does not turn away from the signs of the heavens in the literal sense; the modern person turns away from the signs of the heavens in the sense of the ignorance. The modern person who does not consider the canopy, who does not consider the protection, who does not consider the signs, is the modern person who turns away from the signs, and the turning away is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the refusal is the standing order.

The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the modern person should study the heavens; it is that the modern person should consider the heavens, and the consideration is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the refusal is the standing order. The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the consideration. The reader is meant to examine his own consideration: is the consideration the consideration of the intellect, or is the consideration the consideration of the heart? The sūrah's answer is in the standing order: the consideration is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the refusal is the standing order. The verse, in a single line, is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the protection is the standing order, the signs are the standing order, and the refusal is the standing order.
