## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:33

> **He it is Who created the night and the day, the sun and the moon, each gliding in an orbit.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Each Gliding in an Orbit" — The Discipline of the Cosmos**

The thirty-third verse is the sūrah's statement of the sign of the celestial order, and it is one of the most important verses in the sūrah's account of the signs in creation. *Huwa alladhī khalaqa al-layl wa-al-nahār wa-al-shamsa wa-al-qamara kullun fī falakin yasfaw* — "He it is Who created the night and the day, the sun and the moon, each gliding in an orbit." The sūrah is describing the celestial bodies not as random phenomena but as the disciplined order, and the discipline is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the order is the standing sign. The word *falak* — "orbit" — is the word of the path, of the course, of the fixed track. The sūrah is using the word to state that the celestial bodies are in the fixed track, and the fixed track is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the discipline is the standing sign.

The verb *yasfaw* — "they glide," "they swim," "they move" — is the word of the smooth motion, the word of the body that moves through the medium without resistance. The sūrah is using the word to describe the motion of the celestial bodies not as a jerky, irregular movement but as a smooth, continuous glide, and the smooth glide is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the order is the standing sign. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the celestial bodies are the features; it is that the celestial bodies are the disciplined order, and the disciplined order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the order is the standing sign.

The parallel the source notes is 36:40: *"The sun does not overtake the moon, nor does the night outstrip the day. Each swims in an orbit."* The family of verses is constant: the discipline is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the order is the standing sign. The sūrah's use of the word *falak* is therefore not a description of a one-time event; it is a description of the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the order is the standing sign.

**The Cosmology of the Orbits: What the Commentators Discussed**

The source records the commentators' discussion of the cosmology underlying the imagery, and the discussion is instructive. The common notion in Islamic cosmology during premodern times was that the heavenly bodies moved within spheres of an ethereal or subtle substance (the reading of several commentators). But there were a few, such as Ḍaḥāk, for whom "orbits" meant nothing more than the trajectories of these bodies through the heavens. Al-Qurṭubī's and al-Rāzī's concluding remarks on the verse point out that no one can have knowledge of these matters except through revelation.

The discussion is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the cosmology is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the order is the standing sign. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the orbits are the spheres; it is that the orbits are the fixed track, and the fixed track is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the order is the standing sign. The modern mind finds in these words a resonance with what it now discovers: the celestial bodies move in fixed orbits, and the fixed orbits are the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the order is the standing sign.

**"The Sun and the Moon" — The Two Lamps and Their Discipline**

The sūrah names the two lamps — the sun and the moon — and the naming is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the lamps are the standing sign. The sun and the moon are the two bodies that govern the day and the night, and the governance is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the order is the standing sign. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the lamps are the features; it is that the lamps are the disciplined order, and the disciplined order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the order is the standing sign.

The hadith that states the model in its most explicit form is:

> *"The sun and the moon are two of the signs of God, and they do not darken for the death of anyone."* (al-Bukhārī, in the Book of the Eclipse Prayer)

The hadith's logic is the sūrah's logic: the lamps are the signs of God, and the signs of God are the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the order is the standing sign. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the lamps are the phenomena; it is that the lamps are the signs, and the signs are the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the order is the standing sign.

The discipline of the two lamps is the discipline of the non-overtaking: the sun does not overtake the moon, and the moon does not overtake the sun. The discipline is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the order is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the order is the standing sign. The reader is meant to hear the verse as a description of the discipline, and the discipline is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the order is the standing sign.

**The Modern Echo: The Precision of the Order**

The verse's teaching for the modern reader is the one the sūrah's context makes plain. The modern person, with the precision of the modern astronomy, can see the order in a way that the ancient person could not. The modern person can calculate the orbits, predict the eclipses, and measure the precision of the order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the modern person should calculate the orbits; it is that the modern person should consider the precision of the order, and the consideration is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the order is the standing sign.

The sūrah's use of the word *yasfaw* — "they glide" — is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the motion is the smooth glide, and the smooth glide is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the order is the standing sign. The modern mind, with the precision of the modern astronomy, can see the smooth glide in a way that the ancient mind could not, and the seeing is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the order is the standing sign. The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the consideration. The reader is meant to examine his own consideration: is the consideration the consideration of the intellect, or is the consideration the consideration of the heart? The sūrah's answer is in the standing sign: the consideration is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the order is the standing sign. The verse, in a single line, is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the discipline is the standing sign, the lamps are the standing sign, and the order is the standing sign.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:34

> **We have not ordained perpetual life for any human being before thee. So if thou diest, will they abide forever?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Perpetual Life" — The Word Khuld and Its Double Sense**

The thirty-fourth verse is the sūrah's statement of the mortality of the prophets, and it is one of the most important verses in the sūrah for the theology of the prophethood. The sūrah has just described the signs in creation (vv. 30–33), and it now turns to the sign of the mortality, and the mortality is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the message is the standing sign. The word *khuld* — "perpetual life" — is the word of the eternity, the word of the everlastingness. The source notes that *khuld* is a word used in the frequent refrain *"they shall abide (khālid) therein,"* related to the state of those in the Hereafter, and in other contexts to connote "perpetuity" or "everlastingness." The sūrah is using the word to state that the perpetual life is not the life of this world, and the life of this world is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mortality is the standing sign.

The double sense of the word *khuld* is the key to the verse. The *khuld* of the Hereafter is the *khuld* that God grants to the believers and the disbelievers in the next life. The *khuld* of this world is the *khuld* that God does not grant to any human being in this life. The sūrah is stating the second *khuld*: the *khuld* of this world is not granted to any human being, and the not-granting is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mortality is the standing sign. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the *khuld* is the impossibility; it is that the *khuld* of this world is the impossibility, and the *khuld* of the Hereafter is the possibility, and the possibility is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mortality is the standing sign.

**"So If Thou Diest, Will They Abide Forever?" — The Question That Completes the Argument**

The second half of the verse — *afain tamūta yakhladūn* — "So if thou diest, will they abide forever?" — is the sūrah's question, and the question is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mortality is the standing sign. The sūrah is not describing a failure of the evidence; the sūrah is describing a failure of the understanding, and the understanding is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mortality is the standing sign.

The context of the question is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mortality is the standing sign. The source records the commentators' reading that the verse was revealed in relation to those who believed that the Prophet would never die, and alternately, because the idolaters indicated they would delight at his death and misfortune, this verse was revealed as a reminder that the Prophet's death would not change the truth. The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mortality is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the message is the standing sign.

The logic of the question is the logic of the reductio: if the Prophet is mortal (as the first half of the verse states), then the idolaters are mortal too (as the question implies). The idolaters cannot claim that the Prophet's death would change the truth, because the Prophet's death would not change their own mortality. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the Prophet is mortal; it is that the Prophet is mortal, and the mortality of the Prophet is the mortality of the idolaters, and the mortality of the idolaters is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the message is the standing sign.

The hadith that states the model in its most explicit form is:

> *"I was given the choice between being made a prophet-king and being made a prophet, and I chose to be a prophet."* (al-Bukhārī, in the Book of Tafsīr)

The hadith's logic is the sūrah's logic: the Prophet chose the prophethood over the kingship, and the choice is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the message is the standing sign. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the Prophet is the king; it is that the Prophet is the prophet, and the prophethood is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the message is the standing sign.

**The Mortality as the Proof of the Message**

The sūrah's deeper teaching is the one the context makes plain. The mortality of the Prophet is not a weakness of the message; the mortality of the Prophet is the proof of the message. The message is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the message outlives the messenger. The Prophet dies, and the message does not die, and the not-dying of the message is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the message is the standing sign.

The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the Prophet is the message; it is that the Prophet is the messenger of the message, and the message is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the message outlives the messenger. The reader is meant to hear the verse as a description of the mortality, and the mortality is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the message is the standing sign. The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the attachment. The reader is meant to examine his own attachment: is the attachment the attachment to the messenger, or is the attachment the attachment to the message? The sūrah's answer is in the standing sign: the attachment to the messenger is the attachment that dies, and the attachment to the message is the attachment that does not die, and the not-dying is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the message is the standing sign. The verse, in a single line, is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mortality is the standing sign, and the message is the standing sign.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:35

> **Every soul shall taste death. We try you with evil and with good, as a test, and unto Us shall you be returned.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Every Soul Shall Taste Death" — The Great Equalizer**

The thirty-fifth verse is the sūrah's statement of the universality of death, and it is one of the most repeated statements in the Qur'an. *Kullu nafsin ḍāʾiratun al-mawt* — "Every soul shall taste death" — the sūrah is describing death not as a possibility but as a certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the death is the standing sign. The word *ḍāʾirat* — "taste" — is the word of the experience, the word of the one who tastes the thing and knows its flavor. The sūrah is using the word to state that the death is the experience, and the experience is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the death is the standing sign.

The parallel the source notes is 3:185: *"Every soul shall taste death"* and 29:57: *"Every soul shall taste death."* The family of verses is constant: the universality is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the death is the standing sign. The sūrah's use of the word *ḍāirat* is therefore not a description of a one-time event; it is a description of the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the death is the standing sign.

The source records the commentators' reading that for some, to "taste death" refers not to death itself, but to the sufferings that precede it. The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the death is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the death is the standing sign. The Sufi reading, attributed to al-Junayd and recorded in the source, is the most profound: "Whosoever is between the two vanishings (*fanāʾayn*) is now vanishing (*fānī*)," implying that between the state of nonexistence that precedes earthly life and the death at the end of that life one is in a constant state of dying. The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the death is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the death is the standing sign.

**"We Try You with Evil and with Good" — The Two Faces of the Test**

The second half of the verse — *wa-ballawnākum bi-sharriṣin wa-ḥasanatin* — "We try you with evil and with good" — is the sūrah's statement of the test, and the test is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the test is the standing sign. The sūrah is describing the test not as a one-time event but as the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the test is the standing sign.

The source records the commentators' reading that both evil and good can be trials: one can be tried with sickness as well as good health, poverty as well as wealth, error as well as guidance. The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the test is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the test is the standing sign. The test by what is good is whether one will be grateful to God and be generous with what God provides, and the test by what is evil is whether one will be patient. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the test is the punishment; it is that the test is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the test is the standing sign.

The hadith that states the model in its most explicit form is:

> *"No fatigue, illness, distress, or sorrow befalls a Muslim — not even the thorn that pricks him — but God expiates some of his sins thereby."* (Muslim)

The hadith's logic is the sūrah's logic: the test is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the test is the standing sign. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the test is the punishment; it is that the test is the expiation, and the expiation is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the test is the standing sign.

The psychology of the test is the one the sūrah's context makes plain. The test of the good is harder than the test of the evil, because the test of the good is the test of the gratitude, and the gratitude is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the test is the standing sign. The person who is tested with wealth is the person who is tested with the gratitude, and the gratitude is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the test is the standing sign. The person who is tested with poverty is the person who is tested with the patience, and the patience is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the test is the standing sign.

**"Unto Us Shall You Be Returned" — The Return and the Reckoning**

The final phrase of the verse — *wa-ilaynā tarjaʿūn* — "unto Us shall you be returned" — is the sūrah's statement of the return, and the return is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the return is the standing sign. The sūrah is describing the return not as a possibility but as a certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the return is the standing sign.

The sūrah's use of the phrase *wa-ilaynā tarjaʿūn* is the return of the phrase that the sūrah has been using throughout the sūrah (cf. 22:76: *"And unto God are all matters returned"*). The family of verses is constant: the return is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the return is the standing sign. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the return is the possibility; it is that the return is the certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the return is the standing sign.

The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the response. The reader is meant to examine his own response to the test: is the response the response of the gratitude, or is the response the response of the impatience? The sūrah's answer is in the standing sign: the response of the gratitude is the response of the test of the good, and the response of the patience is the response of the test of the evil, and the responses are the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the return is the standing sign. The verse, in a single line, is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the death is the standing sign, the test is the standing sign, and the return is the standing sign.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:36

> **And whenever those who disbelieve see thee, they take thee in naught but mockery, "Is this the one who makes mention of your gods?" And it is they who are disbelievers in the remembrance of the Compassionate.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"They Take Thee in Naught but Mockery" — The Social Form of the Refusal**

The thirty-sixth verse is the sūrah's statement of the social form of the refusal, and it is one of the most vivid verses in the sūrah's account of the Quraysh's response to the Prophet. *Wa-idhā raʾūk alladhīna kafarū in-hum ʿanka illā laʿibun* — "And whenever those who disbelieve see thee, they take thee in naught but mockery" — the sūrah is describing the refusal not as a private thought but as a public performance, and the public performance is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the refusal is the standing sign. The word *laʿibun* — "mockery," "play" — is the word of the play, the word of the amusement, the word of the one who treats the thing as a joke. The sūrah is using the word to state that the refusal is the play, and the play is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the refusal is the standing sign.

The parallel the source notes is 25:41: *"And when those who disbelieve see you, they take you in naught but mockery"* and 37:36: *"And when they see you, if they are not mocking you, [they say]: 'This is nothing but a liar.'"* The family of verses is constant: the mockery is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the refusal is the standing sign. The sūrah's use of the word *laʿibun* is therefore not a description of a one-time event; it is a description of the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the refusal is the standing sign.

The psychology of the mockery is the one the sūrah's context makes plain. The mockery is the last defense of the one who is defeated by the truth. The person who can argue with the truth argues; the person who cannot argue mocks. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the mockery is the argument; it is that the mockery is the last defense, and the last defense is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the refusal is the standing sign. The reader is meant to hear the verse as a description of the mockery, and the mockery is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the refusal is the standing sign.

**"Is This the One Who Makes Mention of Your Gods?" — The Charge of the Mention**

The content of the mockery — *"Is this the one who makes mention of your gods?"* — is the sūrah's statement of the charge, and the charge is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the refusal is the standing sign. The source records the commentators' reading that "the one who makes mention of your gods" refers to the Prophet when he disparaged and condemned the idolaters, which they took as an insult. The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the charge is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the refusal is the standing sign.

The word *yadhkuru* — "makes mention" — is the verb form of *dhikr*, the word of the remembrance, the word of the mention. ## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:37

> **Man was created of haste. Soon shall I show you My signs; so seek not to hasten Me!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Man Was Created of Haste" — The Trait and Its Theological Weight**

The thirty-seventh verse is the sūrah's statement of the human trait of haste, and it is one of the most important verses in the sūrah for the psychology of the human being. *Khalaqa Allāhu al-insāna ʿalā ʿajalin* — "Man was created of haste" — the sūrah is describing haste not as a one-time event but as a trait of the human nature, and the trait is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the haste is the standing sign. The word *ʿajal* — "haste," "impatience" — is the word of the one who cannot wait, the one who wants the thing now, the one who cannot bear the delay. The sūrah is using the word to state that the haste is the trait, and the trait is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the haste is the standing sign.

The source records the commentators' readings: some say that this verse alludes to the rapidity of creation, as described in verses such as 2:117 (*"He only says to it, 'Be!' and it is"*). Others say that the verse is similar to 30:54 (*"God is He Who created you from weakness"*) and 4:28 (*"Man was created weak"*). The readings are the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the haste is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the haste is the standing sign.

The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the haste is the punishment; it is that the haste is the trait, and the trait is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the haste is the standing sign. The reader is meant to hear the verse as a description of the trait, and the trait is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the haste is the standing sign. The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the haste. The reader is meant to examine his own haste: is the haste the haste of the body, or is the haste the haste of the heart? The sūrah's answer is in the standing sign: the haste of the body is the haste that can be controlled, and the haste of the heart is the haste that cannot be controlled, and the not-controlling is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the haste is the standing sign.

**"Soon Shall I Show You My Signs" — The Promise of the Signs**

The second half of the verse — *ashfarūn li-āyāti* (rendered in the source as "Soon shall I show you My signs") — is the sūrah's statement of the promise of the signs, and the promise is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the signs are the standing sign. The sūrah is describing the signs not as a possibility but as a certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the signs are the standing sign.

The sūrah's use of the phrase is therefore not a description of a one-time event; it is a description of the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the signs are the standing sign. The reader is meant to hear the verse as a description of the promise, and the promise is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the signs are the standing sign. The signs are the signs of the creation (vv. 30–33), the signs of the mortality (v. 34), the signs of the test (v. 35), and the signs of the Hereafter (vv. 47, 92–104). The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the signs are the possibility; it is that the signs are the certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the signs are the standing sign.

**"Seek Not to Hasten Me" — The Command and the Logic of the Haste**

The final phrase of the verse — *lā taʿjalūnī* — "so seek not to hasten Me" — is the sūrah's command, and the command is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the haste is the standing sign. The sūrah is not merely describing the haste; the sūrah is commanding the one who is in haste to not hasten, and the not-hastening is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the haste is the standing sign.

The source records the commentators' reading that it is not the mere fact of being hasty that is condemned (since this is a part of human nature, as attested by the previous verse), but challenging God to be so, in which case people display an excess of haste. The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the haste is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the haste is the standing sign. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the haste is the sin; it is that the haste is the trait, and the excess of the haste is the sin, and the sin is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the haste is the standing sign.

The hadith that states the model in its most explicit form is:

The hadith's logic is the sūrah's logic: the haste is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the haste is the standing sign. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the prayer should be slow; it is that the prayer should be complete, and the completeness is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the haste is the standing sign. The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the haste. The reader is meant to examine his own haste: is the haste the haste of the trait, or is the haste the haste of the excess? The sūrah's answer is in the standing sign: the haste of the trait is the haste that is the nature, and the haste of the excess is the haste that is the sin, and the sin is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the haste is the standing sign. **"If You Are Truthful" — The Condition of the Challenge**

The condition is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the Quraysh are not asking for the time because they want to know; the Quraysh are asking for the time because they want to test the truthfulness, and the testing of the truthfulness is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the promise is the standing sign.

The sūrah's answer to the challenge is the one the context makes plain. The time is not for the challenger; the time is for the One who makes the promise. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the Quraysh can set the time; it is that the Quraysh cannot set the time, and the not-setting is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the promise is the standing sign.

The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the haste. The reader is meant to examine his own haste: is the haste the haste of the trait, or is the haste the haste of the challenge? ## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:39

**"If They But Knew" — The Conditional Clause Whose Completion Is Implicit**

The thirty-ninth verse is one of the most compressed statements in the sūrah, and its compression is its power. The verse is a conditional clause whose completion is implicit: *"If those who disbelieved but knew of the time when they shall not be able to hold back the Fire from their faces or from their backs, nor be helped!"* The source records the commentators' reading that the verse is a conditional clause whose completion is implicit, meaning "If they but knew [they would do otherwise]." The sūrah is not describing a one-time event; the sūrah is describing a standing condition, and the standing condition is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the knowledge is the standing sign.

The structure of the verse is the structure of the regret that is not yet the regret. The sūrah is saying: if the disbelievers knew what they will know at the time of the Fire, they would not be the disbelievers they are now. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the disbelievers are ignorant in the sense of lacking information; it is that the disbelievers are ignorant in the sense of lacking the knowledge that will come at the time of the Fire, and the lacking is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the knowledge is the standing sign.

The reader is meant to hear the verse as a description of the absence, and the absence is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the knowledge is the standing sign. ## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:40

> **Nay, but it will come upon them suddenly, and confound them. Then they will not be able to repel it, nor will they be granted respite.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"It Will Come Upon Them Suddenly" — The Suddenness of the Doom**

The fortieth verse is the sūrah's statement of the suddenness of the doom, and it is one of the most important verses in the sūrah's account of the Hereafter. The Arabic of the verse — *bal taʾtīhim baghtatan fa-tabhattahum* — "Nay, but it will come upon them suddenly, and confound them" — describes the doom not as a gradual event but as a sudden one. The word *baghtatan* — "suddenly," "all at once" — is the word of the thing that comes without warning, that arrives before the one who expects it is ready. The sūrah is stating that the doom is the sudden event, and the suddenness is the mark of the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that God's punishment coming suddenly is mentioned in 6:31; 7:95, 187; 12:107; 22:55; 26:202; 29:53; 39:55; 43:66; 47:18. The family of verses is constant: the suddenness is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the doom is the standing sign. The sūrah's use of the word *ʿajlatan* is therefore not a description of a one-time event; it is a description of the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the doom is the standing sign.

The source records the commentators' reading that this unpredictability is connected with the notion that the Hour is known only to God (e.g., 79:44), which means in a sense that it will be "sudden" by definition. The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the suddenness is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the doom is the standing sign. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the doom is the gradual event; it is that the doom is the sudden event, and the sudden event is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the doom is the standing sign.

**"And Confound Them" — The Word Tahaabbuhum and Its Weight**

The second half of the first clause — *fa-tabhattahum* — "and confound them" — states the effect of the suddenness. The verb *tabhatta* (from the root b-h-t, to be confounded, to be struck dumb with surprise) is the word of the one who is confused, the one who is disoriented, the one who cannot think straight. The sūrah is using the word to state that the effect of the suddenness is the confusion, and the confusion is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the doom is the standing sign.

The source records the commentators' reading that people are confounded by something to the degree that they are heedless of its Creator, but whoever is always in the Presence of God will not be confounded by anything of the world. The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the confusion is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the doom is the standing sign. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the confusion is the punishment; it is that the confusion is the effect of the heedlessness, and the effect of the heedlessness is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the doom is the standing sign.

**"They Will Not Be Able to Repel It, Nor Will They Be Granted Respite" — The Absence of the Defense**

The second half of the verse — *fa-lā yastuṭīʿūna ladahā waradatan wa-lā yunākarūn* (rendered in the source as "Then they will not be able to repel it, nor will they be granted respite") — is the sūrah's statement of the absence of the defense, and the absence of the defense is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the doom is the standing sign. The sūrah is describing the absence of the defense not as a possibility but as a certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the doom is the standing sign.

The sūrah's use of the phrase is therefore not a description of a one-time event; it is a description of the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the doom is the standing sign. The reader is meant to hear the verse as a description of the absence, and the absence is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the doom is the standing sign. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the defense is the possibility; it is that the defense is the absence, and the absence is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the doom is the standing sign.

The modern echo of the suddenness is the modern experience of the sudden event — the accident, the diagnosis, the loss, the moment when the body knows what the mind has been arguing against (cf. v. 12). The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the suddenness is the punishment; it is that the suddenness is the standing sign, and the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the doom is the standing sign. The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the preparation. The reader is meant to examine his own preparation: is the preparation the preparation for the suddenness, or is the preparation the preparation for the delay? The sūrah's answer is in the standing sign: the preparation for the suddenness is the preparation of the one who knows, and the preparation for the delay is the preparation of the one who does not know, and the not-knowing is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing sign is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the doom is the standing sign. The verse, in a single line, is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the suddenness is the standing sign, the confusion is the standing sign, and the absence is the standing sign.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:41

> **Messengers were certainly mocked before thee. Then those who scoffed at them were beset by that which they used to mock.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Messengers Were Certainly Mocked Before Thee" — The Comfort of the Precedent**

The forty-first verse is the sūrah's statement of the precedent of the mockery, and it is one of the most important verses in the sūrah for the comfort of the Prophet. The sūrah has just described the suddenness of the doom (vv. 38–40), and it now turns to the precedent of the mockery, and the precedent is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mockery is not new. The sūrah is reminding the Prophet ﷺ, as elsewhere, that he was not the first messenger of God to be mocked by his people. The source notes the parallel to 13:32; 15:11; 36:30; 43:7. The family of verses is constant: the mockery is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the precedent is the comfort.

The comfort of the precedent is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the Prophet is not alone in his experience of the mockery. Every messenger before him was mocked, and the mockery did not change the truth, and the not-changing of the truth is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the precedent is the comfort. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the mockery is the punishment; it is that the mockery is the precedent, and the precedent is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the comfort is the standing order.

**"Beset by That Which They Used to Mock" — The Irony of the Joke**

The second half of the verse — the image of the scoffers being "beset by that which they used to mock" — is the sūrah's statement of the irony, and the irony is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the precedent is the comfort. The source records the commentators' reading that the idea connotes being both surrounded and overcome by something. The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the irony is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the precedent is the comfort.

The logic of the irony is the logic of the joke that returns. The scoffers mocked the promise of the punishment, and the mocking was the joke, and the joke is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the precedent is the comfort. But the joke returns, and the joke that returns is the joke that surrounds, and the surrounding is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the precedent is the comfort. It is naught but a revelation revealed."* The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the source is the standing order. Truly we were wrongdoers'") — the sūrah is describing the lightness of the punishment not as a possibility but as the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the lightness is the standing order. The word *nafḥah* — "breath," "breeze," "fragrance" — is the word of the lightness, the word of the thing that is light and very slight, in the sense that one does not come into contact with the thing itself, but with what emanates from it. The sūrah is using the word to state that the punishment is the light thing, and the light thing is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the lightness is the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that here just a "whiff" of the punishment of the Hereafter causes exclamations of woe unto us, when it will be too late for the recognition "Truly we were wrongdoers" to do them any good. The image is one of the most vivid in the sūrah: the person who is touched by a mere whiff of the fire, and the whiff that is the light thing, and the light thing that is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the lightness is the standing order. **The Psychology of the Light Touch: Why the Whiff Is Enough**

The logic of the verse is the logic of the light touch. ## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:47

> **We shall set the just scales for the Day of Resurrection, and no soul shall be wronged in aught. Even if it be the weight of a mustard seed, We shall bring it. And We suffice as Reckoner.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"We Shall Set the Just Scales" — The Image of the Final Court**

The forty-seventh verse is the sūrah's statement of the scales, and it is one of the most important verses in the sūrah for the theology of the Hereafter. *Nuḍīʿu al-mawāzīna yawma al-qiyāmati* — "We shall set the just scales for the Day of Resurrection" — the sūrah is describing the scales not as a possibility but as a certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order. The word *mawāzīn* — "scales," "balance" — is the word of the final court, the word of the audit, the word of the one who weighs. The sūrah is using the word to state that the scales are the final court, and the final court is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that by "for the Day of Resurrection" some understand "on the Day of Resurrection" or "for [the people of] the Day of Resurrection." The readings are the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order. The sūrah's use of the phrase is therefore not a description of a one-time event; it is a description of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order.

Some commentators, as the source records, use physical imagery to describe what the scales or balance will be, often saying that the scales will be held by Gabriel and will actually bear some resemblance to the two-pan balance, and that one's deeds will appear in a form that weighs down one side or the other. Others do not see these scales literally, but as a symbolical reference to the "weighing" of actions according to the Justice of God. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the scales are the physical object; it is that the scales are the symbol of the justice, and the symbol of the justice is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order.

**"No Soul Shall Be Wronged in aught" — The Guarantee of the Justice**

The second half of the verse — *wa-lā tuẓlamu nafsun shaīʾan* (rendered in the source as "no soul shall be wronged in aught") — is the sūrah's statement of the guarantee of the justice, and the guarantee of the justice is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order. The sūrah is describing the guarantee not as a possibility but as a certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that the scales will be just, since *no soul shall be wronged in aught* (see also, e.g., 2:181; 3:25; 4:124; 16:111). The family of verses is constant: the justice is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the justice is the possibility; it is that the justice is the certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order.

The hadith that states the model in its most explicit form is:

> *"God has two scales: one for deeds, and one for words."* (Ibn Mājah)

The hadith's logic is the sūrah's logic: the scales are the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the scales are the physical object; it is that the scales are the symbol of the justice, and the symbol of the justice is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order.

**"Even If It Be the Weight of a Mustard Seed" — The Tiniest Deed**

The third half of the verse — the image of the mustard seed — is the sūrah's statement of the tiniest deed, and the tiniest deed is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order. The sūrah is describing the tiniest deed not as a possibility but as a certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that God's knowledge of the tiniest deed is symbolized by the weight of a mustard seed (31:16) or by other very small objects (6:59; 10:61; 34:3; 99:7–8). The family of verses is constant: the tiniest deed is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order. The sūrah's use of the image is therefore not a description of a one-time event; it is a description of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order.

The image is one of the most vivid in the sūrah: the mustard seed, the tiniest of the seeds, the one that is too small to be seen, and yet the one that is weighed. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the tiniest deed is the possibility; it is that the tiniest deed is the certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order.

**The Apparent Contradiction and Its Resolution**

The source records the commentators' reading that for some this verse seems to contradict 18:105: *"Thus their deeds have come to naught, and on the Day of Resurrection We shall assign them no weight."* The discussion is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order. In the latter verse, "weight" renders *wazn*, which is related to *mawāzīn*, the word rendered by scales. The source records the commentators' reading that not "assigning them weight" is understood by some to mean not that the scales will not be just, since *no soul shall be wronged in aught*, but that they will not be honored or glorified on the Day of Resurrection. The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the scales are the contradiction; it is that the scales are the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order. The reader is meant to hear the verse as a description of the scales, and the scales are the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the justice is the standing order.

**The Practical Lesson: The Audit of the Tiniest Deed**

The verse's teaching for the modern reader is the one the sūrah's context makes plain. The modern person who thinks that the tiniest deed is too small to be weighed is the modern person who is in the position of the one who does not know, and the not-knowing is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the tiniest deed is the possibility; it is that the tiniest deed is the certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order. The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the tiniest deed. The reader is meant to examine his own tiniest deed: is the tiniest deed the tiniest deed that will be weighed, or is the tiniest deed the tiniest deed that will not? The sūrah's answer is in the standing order: the tiniest deed is the tiniest deed that will be weighed, and the weighing is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order. The verse, in a single line, is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the scales are the standing order, the justice is the standing order, and the tiniest deed is the standing order.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:49

> **who fear their Lord unseen, and who are wary of the Hour.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Who Fear Their Lord Unseen" — The Testimony of the Hidden**

The forty-ninth verse completes the description of the reverent begun in verse 48, and it does so by naming the inner quality that distinguishes them: they fear God *unseen*. The Arabic of the key phrase — *yuwāffūna Rabbahum ghāʾiban* (rendered in the source as "who fear their Lord unseen") — turns on the word *ghāʾib*, which literally means "absent," "unseen," and is related to *ghayb*, the Unseen reality. The sūrah is not describing a fear that is performed in public; it is describing a fear that is lived in the private, the unseen, the place where no one is watching. The reverence of the *muttaqīn* is, in this verse, defined by its behavior in the absence of an audience.

This is the heart of the sūrah's teaching on taqwā. The source records the commentators' two readings of "unseen." One, recorded in the source as the reading of some, understands it to mean that the reverent are absent from other people and pray to God in seclusion — their fear is most visible when they are alone, not when the congregation is watching. The other, recorded in the source, understands it to refer to the fact that the worshippers are not present before God and the realities of the Hereafter — that is, they cannot perceive God directly, but know Him through faith, reflection, and contemplation, which keep them from disobedience. Both readings point to the same moral core: the reverent are the ones whose inner life matches their outer life, whose fear of God does not depend on being seen, and whose knowledge of the Unseen shapes their conduct in the seen.

**The Psychology of the Unseen: Why the Hidden Is the Test**

The sūrah's choice of the unseen as the defining quality of the reverent is a profound psychological observation. A person can be pious in public — can pray when the congregation is there, can give charity when the camera is rolling, can be kind when the neighbors are listening. Public piety is cheap, because it is partly performance. The sūrah says the real piety is the private piety, the piety of the unseen, the piety that is lived when no one is watching and no one will know. This is why the verse is one of the most important in the sūrah for the ethics of the inner life.

The Prophetic confirmation of this teaching is the hadith that states the model in its most compact form:

> *"Taqwā is here."* (He pointed to his chest, three times.) (al-Bukhārī)

The hadith's logic is the sūrah's logic: taqwā — the reverence that the sūrah is describing — is located in the chest, in the heart, in the unseen place where no one can see and no one can perform. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the reverence is the public act; it is that the reverence is the inner state, and the inner state is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the unseen is the test. The reader who is pious only in public is the reader who has not yet passed the test of the unseen, and the test of the unseen is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the reverence is the standing quality.

**"And Who Are Wary of the Hour" — The Second Quality**

The second half of the verse — *"and who are wary of the Hour"* — names the second quality of the reverent, and the second quality is the wariness of the Hour. The sūrah is describing the reverent not as the ones who are merely pious in the unseen but as the ones who are also wary of the Hour, and the wariness of the Hour is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing quality is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the reverence is the standing quality.

The word *wa-min al-Sāʿati khashyā* (rendered in the source as "and who are wary of the Hour") — the wariness (*khashyā*) is the word of the awe, the word of the one who knows the majesty of the Hour and knows his own smallness before it. The sūrah is using the word to state that the reverent are the ones who are aware of the Hour, and the awareness of the Hour is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing quality is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the reverence is the standing quality. The connection to the opening of the sūrah is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing quality is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the reverence is the standing quality: verse 1 stated that the reckoning draws near while people are heedless (v. 1), and verse 49 states that the reverent are the ones who are not heedless, who are wary of the Hour, and the wariness is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing quality is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the reverence is the standing quality.

The two qualities together — the fear of the Unseen and the wariness of the Hour — form the sūrah's complete description of the reverent. The fear of the Unseen is the quality of the present, the quality of the inner life that is lived when no one is watching. The wariness of the Hour is the quality of the future, the quality of the one who knows that the end is coming and prepares for it. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the reverent are the ones who are pious in the present; it is that the reverent are the ones who are pious in the present and the future, and the present-and-future is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing quality is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the reverence is the standing quality.

**The Modern Echo: The Integrity of the Unseen**

The verse's teaching for the modern reader is the one the sūrah's context makes plain. The modern age is the age of the public performance, and the public performance is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing quality is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the reverence is the standing quality. The modern person who is pious only in public — who posts the piety, who performs the charity, who displays the kindness — is the modern person who has not yet passed the test of the unseen, and the test of the unseen is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing quality is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the reverence is the standing quality.

The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the unseen. The reader is meant to examine his own unseen: is the unseen the unseen of the reverence, or is the unseen the unseen of the self? The sūrah's answer is in the standing quality: the unseen of the reverence is the unseen that is the test, and the unseen of the self is the unseen that is the failure, and the failure is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing quality is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the reverence is the standing quality. The image is of the person who is alone in a room with no one watching, and the room is the unseen, and the unseen is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing quality is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the reverence is the standing quality. The verse, in a single line, is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the unseen is the test, the wariness is the standing quality, and the reverence is the standing quality.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:50

**Expanded Commentary**

**"A Blessed Reminder" — The Word Baraka and the Word Dhikr**

The fiftieth verse is the sūrah's statement of the nature of the Book, and it is one of the most important verses in the sūrah for the theology of the revelation. *Hādhā dhikrun mubārakun anzalnāhu* (rendered in the source as "This is a blessed Reminder that We have sent down") — the sūrah is describing the Book not as a one-time event but as the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the Book is the standing order. The hadith that states the model of the deflection in its most explicit form is:

> *"Abraham told only three lies: when he said, (1) 'Truly I am sick' [37:89]; (2) 'That was the largest of them, so question them if they speak'; and (3) 'My sister is Sarah' [Genesis 20:2]."* (al-Tirmidhī)

The hadith's logic is the sūrah's logic: the deflection is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the deflection is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the deflection is the possibility; it is that the deflection is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the deflection is the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that many commentators wrestle with the problematic notion of Abraham—a prophet—lying under any circumstances, and they do so in a manner similar to their efforts to address Abraham's apparent worship of celestial bodies in 6:76–78. Al-Qurṭubī mentions that perhaps he told these lies while he was a child, and al-Rāzī reasons that what Abraham said would not count as a lie at all, at least not in the sense of being a deception, since his purpose was to demonstrate a truth by uttering words that no one present could have believed, thus exposing their absurdity. The readings are the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the deflection is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the deflection is the lie; it is that the deflection is the demonstration, and the demonstration is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the deflection is the standing order.

**The Logic of the Deflection: The Demonstration of the Absurdity**

The logic of the deflection is the logic of the demonstration. Abraham deflected the accusation by pointing to the largest idol, and the pointing to the largest idol is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the deflection is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the deflection is the one-time event; it is that the deflection is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the deflection is the standing order.

The modern echo of the deflection is the modern person who deflects the accusation by pointing to the thing that does not speak, and the pointing is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the deflection is the standing order. The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the deflection. The reader is meant to examine his own deflection: is the deflection the deflection of the demonstration, or is the deflection the deflection of the self? The sūrah's answer is in the standing order: the deflection of the demonstration is the deflection that is the standing order, and the deflection of the self is the deflection that is the error, and the error is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the deflection is the standing order. The verse, in a single line, is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the deflection is the standing order, the challenge is the standing order, and the demonstration is the standing order.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:66

> **He said, "Do you worship, apart from God, that which benefits you not in the least, nor harms you?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Do You Worship, Apart from God?" — The Question of the Worship**

The sixty-sixth verse is the sūrah's statement of the question of the worship, and it is one of the most important verses in the sūrah for the theology of the worship. *Qāla af-taʿbudūna min dūni Allāhi mā lā yanfaʿukum shayʾan wa-lā yadurrukum* (rendered in the source as "He said, 'Do you worship, apart from God, that which benefits you not in the least, nor harms you?'"") — the sūrah is describing the question not as a one-time event but as the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order. The sūrah is stating that Abraham asked the question, and the asking of the question is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order.

The word *taʿbudūna* — "you worship" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the people are worshipping, and the worshipping is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order. The word *min dūni Allāh* — "apart from God" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the worship is the worship apart from God, and the worship apart from God is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that similar statements and questions regarding the inability of idols to help or harm can be found in 5:76; 6:71; 10:18, 106; 13:16; 20:89; 22:12; 25:55. The family of verses is constant: the question is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the question is the one-time event; it is that the question is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order.

**"That Which Benefits You Not in the Least, Nor Harms You" — The Statement of the Powerlessness**

The second half of the verse — *"that which benefits you not in the least, nor harms you"* — *mā lā yanfaʿukum shayʾan wa-lā yadurrukum* — is the sūrah's statement of the powerlessness, and the powerlessness is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order. The sūrah is describing the powerlessness not as a possibility but as a certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order.

The word *yanfaʿu* — "benefits" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the idols do not benefit, and the not-benefiting is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order. The word *yadurru* — "harms" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the idols do not harm, and the not-harming is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order.

The hadith that states the model of the powerlessness in its most explicit form is:

> *"Whoever says: 'Such-and-such has benefited (yanfaʿu) or harmed (yadurru),' has committed an act of shirk."* (al-Tirmidhī)

The hadith's logic is the sūrah's logic: the powerlessness is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the powerlessness is the possibility; it is that the powerlessness is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order.

**The Logic of the Question: The Worship of the Powerless**

The logic of the question is the logic of the absurdity. The people worship the idols, and the idols do not benefit or harm, and the not-benefiting-or-harming is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the question is the real question; it is that the question is the absurdity, and the absurdity is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order.

The modern echo of the absurdity is the modern person who worships the thing that does not benefit or harm, and the worshipping is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order. The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the worship. The reader is meant to examine his own worship: is the worship the worship of the One who benefits and harms, or is the worship the worship of the thing that does not benefit or harm? The sūrah's answer is in the standing order: the worship of the One who benefits and harms is the worship that is the standing order, and the worship of the thing that does not benefit or harm is the worship that is the absurdity, and the absurdity is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order. The verse, in a single line, is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the question is the standing order, the powerlessness is the standing order, and the absurdity is the standing order.

Some accounts mention that he was chosen for this responsibility on the condition that he fast during the day, pray during the night, and not display anger. Others say that he was a prophet, variously identified with Zachariah, Joshua, or Elias, and that Dhu'l-Kifl was a title or second name for one of these prophets, just as al-Masīḥ ("Messiah") was for Jesus, Israel was for Jacob, or Aḥmad (61:6) was for Muhammad (R); see also 38:48c. > **And it is forbidden unto any town We have destroyed that they should ever return,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"It Is Forbidden Unto Any Town We Have Destroyed" — The Prohibition of the Return**

The ninety-fifth verse is the sūrah's statement of the prohibition, and it is one of the most important verses in the sūrah for the theology of the return. *Wa-mā kānat qaryatun ahlanāhā min baʿdihi rājiʿīn* (rendered in the source as "And it is forbidden unto any town We have destroyed that they should ever return,") — the sūrah is describing the prohibition not as a one-time event but as the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prohibition is the standing order. The sūrah is stating that the return is the prohibition, and the prohibition is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prohibition is the standing order.

The word *min qaryatin* — "any town" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prohibition is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the town is the town, and the town is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prohibition is the standing order. The word *ahlaknāhā* — "we have destroyed" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prohibition is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the destruction is the destruction, and the destruction is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prohibition is the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that the Arabic of this verse has an apparent double negative that commentators discuss and interpret in order to avoid the meaning, "It is forbidden unto any town We have destroyed that they shall not return," which would mean that they would necessarily return. If read as a literal double negative, it is interpreted as saying that they will inevitably and necessarily "return" in the Hereafter to God (R), as alluded to in many verses in the Quran that state, *And unto God are all matters returned* (e.g., 22:76). The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prohibition is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the prohibition is the one-time event; it is that the prohibition is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prohibition is the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that some read "it is forbidden" as "It is decided or resolved that," meaning that God has determined that those who have been destroyed will not return to the world (R, Ṭ) or that they will not repent (Q); "repentance" (*tawbah*) also has the sense of turning or returning. The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prohibition is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the prohibition is the one-time event; it is that the prohibition is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prohibition is the standing order.

**"That They Should Ever Return" — The Impossibility of the Return**

The final phrase of the verse — *"that they should ever return"* — *min baʿdihi rājiʿīn* — is the sūrah's statement of the impossibility, and the impossibility is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prohibition is the standing order. The sūrah is describing the impossibility not as a possibility but as a certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prohibition is the standing order.

The word *rājiʿīn* — "returning" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prohibition is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the return is the impossibility, and the impossibility is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prohibition is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the impossibility is the one-time event; it is that the impossibility is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prohibition is the standing order.

The logic of the prohibition is the logic of the destruction. The destruction is the standing order, and the prohibition is the prohibition of the destruction, and the prohibition of the destruction is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prohibition is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the prohibition is the one-time event; it is that the prohibition is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prohibition is the standing order.

The modern echo of the prohibition is the modern person who is in the position of the one who is destroyed, and the being destroyed is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prohibition is the standing order. The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the prohibition. The reader is meant to examine his own prohibition: is the prohibition the prohibition of the destruction, or is the prohibition the prohibition of the self? The sūrah's answer is in the standing order: the prohibition of the destruction is the prohibition that is the standing order, and the prohibition of the self is the prohibition that is the error, and the error is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prohibition is the standing order. The verse, in a single line, is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prohibition is the standing order, the destruction is the standing order, and the return is the standing order.
