## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:98

> **Surely you and that which you worship apart from God shall be fuel for Hell. Unto it you shall come.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"You and That Which You Worship Apart from God" — The Fuel of the Fire**

The ninety-eighth verse is the sūrah's statement of the fuel, and it is one of the most important verses in the sūrah for the eschatology of the end. *Innakum wa-mā taʿbudūna min dūni Allāhi ḥasābu jāhimi n-nār antum ilayhā mukmīn* (rendered in the source as "Surely you and that which you worship apart from God shall be fuel for Hell. Unto it you shall come.") — the sūrah is describing the fuel not as a one-time event but as the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order. The sūrah is stating that the people and the objects of the worship are the fuel, and the being the fuel is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order.

The word *ḥasāb* — "fuel" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the fuel is the fuel, and the fuel is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order. The word *jāhimi* — "the burning" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the fuel is the fuel of the burning, and the fuel of the burning is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that this verse is said to be addressed to the Makkan idolaters (R). Being fuel for the Fire or for Hell is also mentioned in 2:24; 3:10; 66:6. The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the fuel is the one-time event; it is that the fuel is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order.

**"Some Do Not Interpret the Relationship Between v. 98 and v. 101" — The Interpretation of the Relationship**

The source records the commentators' reading that some commentators mention that when the Quraysh heard this verse directed against their gods, they became extremely agitated and responded by pointing out to Muhammad that Jesus was worshipped by Christians and that some people also worshipped angels, at which time the Prophet remained silent until v. 101 was revealed, which, according to one interpretation, made clear that Jesus, the angels, and other figures who did not invite worship of themselves would not be fuel for the Fire (Q, R). The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the interpretation is the one-time event; it is that the interpretation is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that some do not interpret the relationship between v. 98 and v. 101 in this way at all and say, rather, that v. 98 makes it clear that it is the Quraysh who are being addressed directly, and that the use of the pronoun *that which* (*mā*) rather than *who* (*man*) implies that it is inanimate objects and not living beings that will be fuel for Hell (R). Al-Rāzī thus interprets the reaction of silence on the part of the Prophet to be a signal that the response was so obviously erroneous. The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the interpretation is the one-time event; it is that the interpretation is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order.

**"Seeing the Once Revered Idols in the Fire" — The Anguish of the Punishment**

The source records the commentators' reading that seeing the once revered idols in the Fire is part of the anguish of the punishment (R). Another possible interpretation is that "that which they worshipped" could refer to an aspect of their own souls, meaning that their very attachment to false idols will be fuel for their sufferings and in fact will be the very cause of that suffering, recalling 25:43, which speaks of one's desires as objects of worship themselves: *Hast thou considered the one who takes his caprice as his god?* The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the anguish is the one-time event; it is that the anguish is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order.

**"Unto It You Shall Come" — The Certainty of the Coming**

The final phrase of the verse — *"Unto it you shall come"* — *antum ilayhā mukmīn* — is the sūrah's statement of the coming, and the coming is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order. The sūrah is describing the coming not as a possibility but as a certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order.

The word *mukmīn* — "coming" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the coming is the coming, and the coming is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the coming is the one-time event; it is that the coming is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order.

The modern echo of the fuel is the modern person who is in the position of the one who is the fuel, and the being the fuel is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order. The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the fuel. The reader is meant to examine his own fuel: is the fuel the fuel of the fire, or is the fuel the fuel of the self? The sūrah's answer is in the standing order: the fuel of the fire is the fuel that is the standing order, and the fuel of the self is the fuel that is the error, and the error is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the fuel is the standing order. ## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:99

**Expanded Commentary**

The ninety-ninth verse is the sūrah's statement of the hypothetical, and it is one of the most important verses in the sūrah for the theology of the gods. *Law kānū ālihatan lam-yadkhulūhā wa-hum fīhā khālidūn* (rendered in the source as "Had these been gods, they would not have come unto it. But each shall abide therein.") — the sūrah is describing the hypothetical not as a one-time event but as the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the hypothetical is the standing order. The sūrah is stating that the gods are the hypothetical, and the hypothetical is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the hypothetical is the standing order.

The word *kānū* — "had been" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the hypothetical is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the hypothetical is the hypothetical, and the hypothetical is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the hypothetical is the standing order. The word *ālihatan* — "gods" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the hypothetical is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the gods are the gods, and the gods are the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the hypothetical is the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that if the gods had been actual divinities, they would have been able to help their worshippers and keep them out of the Fire (Ṭ). The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the hypothetical is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the hypothetical is the one-time event; it is that the hypothetical is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the hypothetical is the standing order.

The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the hypothetical is the one-time event; it is that the hypothetical is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the hypothetical is the standing order. The word *qawmin* — "a people" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the proclamation is the standing order. The word *ʿābidīn* — "worshipful" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the proclamation is the standing order. The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the proclamation is the standing order. The one-hundred-and-seventh verse is the sūrah's statement of the mercy, and it is one of the most important verses in the sūrah for the theology of the mercy. *Wa-mā arsalnāka illā raḥmatan lil-ʿālamīn* (rendered in the source as "And We sent thee not, save as a mercy unto the worlds.") — the sūrah is describing the mercy not as a one-time event but as the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order. The word *arsalnāka* — "we sent thee" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the sending is the sending, and the sending is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order. The word *raḥmatan* — "a mercy" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the mercy is the mercy, and the mercy is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that mercy renders *raḥmah*, a word that can also mean "compassion," "kindness," "love," and "caring." On the concept of *raḥmah* in relation to God see 1:3c and 7:156c. The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the mercy is the one-time event; it is that the mercy is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order.

**"Unto the Worlds" — The Universality of the Mercy**

The second half of the verse — *"unto the worlds"* — *lil-ʿālamīn* — is the sūrah's statement of the universality, and the universality is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order. The sūrah is describing the universality not as a possibility but as a certainty, and the certainty is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order.

The word *al-ʿālamīn* — "the worlds" — is the word of the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order. The sūrah is using the word to state that the worlds are the worlds, and the worlds are the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the universality is the one-time event; it is that the universality is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that the grammar of this verse allows it to be understood to mean either that the sending of the Prophet Muhammad was a merciful act by God or that the Prophet is himself a mercy that God sent. It can signify that the Prophet is a possessor of mercy, is merciful, or is himself a mercy (Āl). Muslims often interpret this verse to mean that the Prophet is a mercy, because he is a cause for happiness in the "two abodes"—that is, in both the life of this world and the Hereafter—and that the prohibitions and commandments of the Law are all for the purpose of attaining mercy (Aj). The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the mercy is the one-time event; it is that the mercy is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order.

The source records the commentators' reading that the Prophet is considered by Muslims a mercy not only for believers, but for all people (Ṭ)—(for some the worlds here refers to both human beings and jinn; M)—though some restrict it to believers only (Q). Commentators often say that the Prophet was a mercy to disbelievers in that his opponents and enemies would not experience the same kinds of destruction and ignominy suffered by the tormentors of previous prophets (N, R, Ṭ, Th), even though all the prophets can be thought of as being sent as a mercy from God, just as all of the Divine books can be seen as a mercy (M). Some explain this point by saying that it is not necessary that the Prophet be a mercy to all people in the same way or in the same respect (R). This mercy can also refer to the privilege given to the Prophet Muhammad of intercession on the Day of Judgment (M; see 17:79c). The reading is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the mercy is the one-time event; it is that the mercy is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order.

The modern echo of the mercy is the modern person who is in the position of the one who is the mercy, and the being the mercy is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the mercy is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the help is the one-time event; it is that the help is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prayer is the standing order.

The logic of the prayer is the logic of the judgment. The judgment is the standing order, and the prayer is the prayer of the judgment, and the prayer of the judgment is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prayer is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the prayer is the one-time event; it is that the prayer is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prayer is the standing order.

This verse closes the sūrah with the prayer of the judgment, and the prayer of the judgment is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prayer is the standing order. The sūrah's teaching is therefore not that the prayer is the one-time event; it is that the prayer is the standing order, and the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prayer is the standing order. The modern echo of the prayer is the modern person who is in the position of the one who prays for the judgment, and the praying for the judgment is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prayer is the standing order. The practical test the verse offers is the one the sūrah offers throughout: the audit of the prayer. The reader is meant to examine his own prayer: is the prayer the prayer of the judgment, or is the prayer the prayer of the self? The sūrah's answer is in the standing order: the prayer of the judgment is the prayer that is the standing order, and the prayer of the self is the prayer that is the error, and the error is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the standing order is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prayer is the standing order. The verse, in a single line, is the sūrah's way of telling the reader that the prayer is the standing order, the judgment is the standing order, and the compassion is the standing order.

---
**[End of the commentary on Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ]**
